The Atlantic
Another Big Difference Between Liberals and Conservatives
2026-08-20

Another Big Difference Between Liberals and Conservatives

The Atlantic | 2026-08-20

Visit Original Website
When some Taylor Swift fans listened to the lyrics of her 2025 song “Wi$h Li$t,” in which she sings about yearning to “settle down” with “a couple kids,” they heard a Republican dog whistle. On social media, they claimed that Swift’s desire for family life was “a conservative agenda on a silver platter.” Swift, an avowed feminist who has endorsed Democratic political candidates, almost certainly did not set out to make “tradwife propaganda.” But her online critics are getting at something real: Having children—particularly lots of children—has become a bit right-coded, as the kids say. Americans and Europeans who identify as leftist or liberal tend to have fewer children than those who identify as conservative or right-leaning. In the United States, differences in desired fertility seem to start at a young age—one 2024 study found that Republican-identifying 12th graders said they want more children than Democrat-identifying 12th graders. Of course, plenty of liberals have children, and plenty of conservatives have small families or remain childless. And many Americans identify as neither conservative nor liberal, but rather as something in between. Still, several academics told me that research conducted in the past few years has shown a fairly strong association between ideology and fertility. The latest evidence comes from a June report by the Institute for Family Studies, a think tank that promotes marriage and family life, which polled more than 7,000 Americans aged 18 to 54 and found that self-identified conservatives had 1.4 kids, on average, to liberals’ 1.09, a statistically significant difference. The study also found that liberals were more likely to not have children. The ideological fertility gap remained even after controlling for other variables that may influence family size, such as religion, race, income, age, marital status, and education. Lyman Stone, the head of IFS’s pronatalism initiative and the researcher who ran the poll, told me that when he first saw the results, he thought, Duh. This trend of greater fertility on the right fits with a pattern that he and other demographers have noticed since the 1990s. There’s no clear explanation for why, precisely, liberals tend to have fewer children than conservatives do. Causation and correlation are difficult to untangle here. Liberals are more likely to pursue higher education and to marry later, both of which can lead to having fewer children, and some research suggests that having children may make a person become more socially conservative. The researchers I spoke with put forth three general theories about why the partisan fertility gap may persist, beyond simple demographics. First, liberals seem to have more anxiety about both the state of the world and their own parenting abilities, which may make them more reluctant to start a family. Second, the values and aspirations of liberals and conservatives tend to differ in ways that shape how they choose to live their lives. Finally, for decades, liberals and conservatives have been sorting into disparate geographic locations and lifestyles, and their respective bubbles seem to reinforce the decision to have children, or not. The result is that a singer such as Swift expressing her desire to have children may, to some liberals, seem like she’s spinning propaganda for a traditional life they can’t imagine wanting. Some of the most striking data points from the IFS survey suggest that liberals hold a bleaker view of parenthood than conservatives do. Among liberals, 18 percent of respondents were unsure whether they would be a good parent, compared with 9 percent of conservatives. More than a third of the liberals said they thought parenting is “very complicated, difficult, and stressful,” compared with less than a quarter of conservatives. And 19 percent of liberals thought their mental health was “not good enough to have children right now,” compared with 10 percent of conservatives. All three beliefs were associated with having fewer children. “These views of parenting shape your idea of how hard and how rewarding it’s going to be to be a parent,” Stone said. Other researchers I interviewed suggested several factors that might lead liberals to have this grim perspective. One is that liberals are more likely to follow a broad array of mainstream news sources, which may make them more aware of societal problems such as climate change or rising authoritarianism, and thus more concerned about bringing children into a world with such seemingly intractable challenges. Keeping up with current events could also alert liberals to changes in state policies that make pregnancy riskier. “The media that you consume, is it telling you that you might not be able to get pregnancy care if you need it?” Karen Guzzo, a demographer at the University of North Carolina at Chapel Hill who studies family formation, asked me. According to Guzzo, liberals may also be more likely to believe that daunting news events will have a measurable impact on their life. Conservatives, by contrast, tend to believe that they will persevere despite whatever happens in the world. Many studies have shown that conservatives tend to favor individual responsibility and free will as an explanation for people’s successes and failures. When faced with a hypothetical scenario of a person losing their job, for example, conservatives are more likely to assume that the person was a poor performer, rather than that the company had financial problems. Guzzo said that many liberals, by contrast, are more likely to believe that structural factors such as the economy or the housing market will shape their future. Some researchers also wondered whether therapeutic ideas about trauma and family dynamics could play a role in discouraging liberals from having kids. Liberals are more likely than conservatives to seek out counseling, and one view that can emerge in therapy is that the client’s own parents damaged them in some way. If someone believes “my parents have harmed me,” Heather Rackin, a Louisiana State University sociologist who studies family formation, told me, “they might become really afraid of reproducing those harms on their children.” Another factor possibly driving the fertility gap is that Democrats and Republicans, as a whole, have different values. Researchers told me that many Democrats believe that the pinnacle of achievement is having a good education and a fruitful career. For many Republicans, the primary goal in life is to raise children. Conservatives, Stone said, “tend to just be predisposed to think that family is what you’re supposed to do.” Conservatives and liberals, Rackin said, seem to have different answers to the questions “What does it mean to be an adult?” and “What does it mean to live a good life?” According to various  studies and surveys, she said, “conservatives are more likely to see people as embedded within traditions and obligations and social roles, and parenthood is a role.” Liberals, Rackin added, seem more interested in self-definition, and in “constructing and expressing your own identity and aspirations. You don’t just accept roles because of tradition.” In one recent Pew Research Center poll, for instance, Republicans were more likely than Democrats to say that having children was “highly important” for living a fulfilling life, and in another, Democrats were more likely than Republicans to say that creative activities were “one of the most important things” to them. Democrats, then, may be less willing to choose parenthood when it competes as a priority with career, friendships, travel, and other pursuits. Read: The best parenting hack is being rich Religion explains much of this values gap. Some 80 percent of conservatives are Christian, for example, compared with 37 percent of liberals, and many denominations of Christianity encourage having children. But even in studies that control for religiosity, some of the difference in desired fertility between the parties remains. Something about the conservative worldview seems oriented toward family life. “I think even if you took nonreligious conservatives and nonreligious liberals and compared them,” Rackin said, they’d still have different definitions of the meaning of life, which would in turn give them different perspectives on whether to have children. The final explanation for the partisan fertility gap stems from the fact that, over the past few decades, the two major political parties have become more and more geographically sorted. Democrats tend to live among Democrats; Republicans, among Republicans. Jacob Brown, a Boston University political scientist who studies this phenomenon, told me this doesn’t necessarily mean Republicans and Democrats physically moved away from each other. Instead, the two parties shifted over time such that they have come to represent different types of people—the Democratic Party appeals to people in cities; the Republican Party targets rural voters. Now people who belong to each party disproportionately socialize with other people of the same party. Less than a third of Democrats, according to one recent survey, have “at least some” Republican friends. This sorting means that initially small differences in fertility between liberals and conservatives can compound over time. As liberals see many other liberal, child-free people around them, and conservatives see fellow conservatives with larger families, they may make decisions about their own family size based on the choices of their friends and neighbors. “You’re getting cues about what appropriate behavior is from those around you,” Rackin said. Research from the Penn State sociologist Daniel DellaPosta shows how this kind of social reinforcement can work. In a 2015 study, DellaPosta and his colleagues examined a survey of 216 different activities and beliefs—such as watching a dance performance or reading horoscopes—and found that 58 percent of them were significantly correlated with political ideology. People who dismissed modern art as something “a child could do,” for example, tended to be conservative, whereas jazz aficionados were likely to be liberal. This isn’t because liberal psychology necessarily fosters a love for John Coltrane. Rather, liberals and conservatives tend to interact with people similar to themselves, and through those exchanges, they influence one another’s lifestyle, DellaPosta told me. A liberal jazz fan, in other words, will spread his enthusiasm for A Love Supreme to other liberals because his social network is likely to be made up mostly of liberals. From the March 2020 issue: The nuclear family was a mistake Something similar could be happening with decisions about whether to have children, or how many to have. “Political identity and all kinds of lifestyle preferences can become very closely aligned even when there’s no necessary or intrinsic connection between them,” DellaPosta told me. (He’s not the only one who’s found these associations: In a 2018 study, two researchers found that liberals are, in fact, more likely to drink lattes than conservatives are.) Of course, some behaviors and beliefs do have an obvious connection to politics. Conservatives in DellaPosta’s study were more likely to endorse spanking kids, for instance, which aligns with conservatism’s more traditionalist worldview. Liberals were more likely to say that people who intend to get married should live together first, which corresponds with liberalism’s more sexually permissive mores. But DellaPosta’s work also shows that political preferences may influence decisions about having children not because there’s something Republican about having kids, but because political affiliation invisibly guides how people live their life. Maybe no single one of these theories is right—but, rather, all of them are. Ideology may nudge people into parenthood or childlessness by subtly shaping what they value, what they fear, whom they surround themselves with, and what they want out of life. The end result is that, as everyone settles on one side of the parenthood fence or the other, many pick the side with people a lot like themselves.
The Atlantic
Secrets of a Jeopardy Champion
2026-08-20

Secrets of a Jeopardy Champion

The Atlantic | 2026-08-20

Visit Original Website
Subscribe here: Apple Podcasts | Spotify | YouTube | Overcast | Pocket Casts I remember each of my triumphs in a trivia game because they are so rare. (Highlight, 2023: Who is Barry Manilow?) I do not shine in bar-trivia night, or charades, or Sporcle. At home, my track record when shouting out Jeopardy answers was always worse than anyone else’s on the couch, including my own children, who are obviously much younger than I am. What is Walden? What is Mount Everest? What is a pansy? Whatever comes at the end of “What is,” I usually do not know. The children long ago moved on to the British quiz show University Challenge, which I watched only once and never again, to preserve my sense of authority as a parent and also my self-worth (“If the number of the year of the Battle of Hastings is divided by the number of wives of Henry VIII, which pope’s numerical designation do you get?”) I’ve always comforted myself by saying that trivia was a pointless accumulation of junk in the brain, the mental equivalent of a cluttered closet. But this is obviously just envy. Knowing trivia is a skill like any other. It can be learned, practiced, and serve you well in many ways beyond shouting answers to the TV. On this episode of Radio Atlantic: Drew Goins—an Atlantic staff writer, a Jeopardy champion, and the creator of The Atlantic’s new trivia game, Rabbithole—shares his secrets on winning at Jeopardy and cultivating a very particular brand of curiosity. The following is a transcript of the episode: Hanna Rosin: I have never had the desire to go on Jeopardy. Like, at all. And to be clear, I haven’t been invited either. Alex Trebek: If you’re ready, then let us play Jeopardy. Rosin: My track record when I’m sitting on the couch, shouting out answers, is not good. Rosin: I don’t have the patience or the talent to memorize trivia, and I don’t crave the spotlight or pressure that comes with it. But recently, I started to feel like even though I don’t want to be a Jeopardy champion, it might be worth it to start thinking like one. Drew Goins is a senior editor at The Atlantic. He created The Atlantic’s new trivia game, Rabbithole. But more importantly for this conversation and his Instagram following, he’s also a Jeopardy champion. Ken Jennings: Drew Goins, congratulations! $35,000 for you, and you’re— Rosin: And his approach to the game and to championdom is not what I expected, which is: Just be a born genius. Turns out, it helps to be curious. Be the kind of person who finds joy in knowledge for knowledge’s sake, which seems like a quality that could be good for life, not just Jeopardy. Also, there are some tricks, secrets that only a champion would know. Drew Goins: The key to winning at Jeopardy is not knowing everything; the key to winning at Jeopardy is knowing one thing about everything. Rosin: Today on the show: how to win at Jeopardy. Rosin: I’m very honored to have a Jeopardy champion. Goins: A Jeopardy champion and a Jeopardy loser—let’s not forget that. Rosin: So what was the first moment when you saw Jeopardy and thought, That’s me. I wanna do that. That speaks to me? Goins: I think this is gonna be a common answer for people of my age, which is: the original run of Ken Jennings. He is obviously the host now. He won 74 games before that in the mid-aughts. And I remember watching and thinking, Oh my God, you know, this dweeby software engineer is the coolest person I have ever seen. Ken will say he’s still kind of amazed that people find him cool or whatever. But I have this very vivid childhood memory of being on a family beach vacation and scurrying back in from the ocean because it was almost 7 o’clock, and Ken was still playing, and Jeopardy comes on at 7:00. And that day at the, you know, boardwalk, I had won some inflatable raft to take into the ocean, and I spent the episode blowing it up with my mouth. We did not have a pump or anything. And so by the end of the episode, I was so lightheaded from blowing up this raft. And then Ken loses. Trebek: —FedEx. His wager was $5,601. He winds up in second place with $8,799. Trebek: All good things have to come to an end, don’t they? Well, too bad for Ken, but he’s going home with a lot of money. Goins: And I’m, like, a middle schooler at the time. And I’m like, Oh, surely, I’m hallucinating. It must be the lack of oxygen in my brain. This can’t be happening. (Laughs.) Goins: And then I asked my dad, “Did that really happen?” And he said, “Son, I’m afraid so.” I went on to avenge the legacy of Ken Jennings. Rosin: So how did you go about studying? Like, how does one actually study? I feel like I’ve seen documentaries about spelling-bee kids, so I do have a sense of what their life looks like. What does a Jeopardy kid’s life look like? Goins: The spelling-bee comparison I think is gonna be elucidating because when these kids are prepping for the Scripps, they have a really discrete amount of information to learn. Obviously, the English language is very generative. There are tons and tons and tons of words, so it’s strange to say discrete, but they learn their roots, and they learn various suffixes and the way that morphemes combine to form words. Jeopardy is different, at least at first blush, ’cause it feels like Jeopardy can ask about anything. And so it’s a really overwhelming thing to set out and actually prepare for Jeopardy. I think the first step of it, at least in my experience, happened just in my upbringing, before I was actively preparing for the show, which is just a lifetime of curiosity. Because it does ask about so much—it just pays to be a really curious person who’s interested in a lot of things. But then once you get what’s called The Call (capital T, capital C)—that’s what Jeopardy people say when you actually get the invitation to come onto the show—that’s kind of when you kick into high gear for studying, studying. And you realize pretty quickly once you set out to actually learn Jeopardy stuff, that the canon of what Jeopardy asks about and the depth at which Jeopardy asks things is pretty much the same as the spelling bee. Like, there are tricks to pick up, there are bounds to what you need to learn, and you just need to figure out what those bounds are. Once you go on the show, if you happen to be invited back and do well, people say you kind of get stuck in “forever Jeopardy.” So I might go back again at some point. I’ve gone a couple of times to play Jeopardy. But now that I’m in that space, every day I have an app on my phone that has thousands of flash cards on it. And I am hitting new flash cards to remember what the currency of the United Arab Emirates is, or remember what Pascalization is—these things that you just accrete over time that will disappear if you don’t tend to them. Rosin: Okay, so now I’m in the other world with you. We’ve moved through the portal, and as you’re in this portal and you’re accumulating this knowledge, what are some taxonomies that you have developed? Like, what is the world of Jeopardy? What’s the landscape? What does it look like? What are you learning? Goins: I think what Jeopardy really celebrates and what the Jeopardy canon really celebrates is an old-fashioned idea of a well-rounded person. The example that I think of and that a lot of people point to is how much Jeopardy asks about opera. Jennings: It’s time for Final Jeopardy, where we’re getting a little high-brow today. “Opera Source Material” is the category. Goins: The average Joe Schmo, myself included, before going on Jeopardy does not know a whole lot about opera. But once you hit the Double Jeopardy round, which is where things become a little more rarefied, a little more academic, none of this pop-culture smut that appears in the first round of Jeopardy, you will often get questions about opera. If you want to know whether something is likely to appear in the Jeopardy canon, ask yourself at a dinner party of a dozen or so well-read guests, Would two or more people know the thing? Jeopardy has pushed me to want to be a better, more knowledgeable person, including learning things that I didn’t know before. Sports, for instance: I am not much of a sports person, and Jeopardy asks a lot about it. And I think what I tell myself when learning about sports or doing flash cards, It’s like taking my vitamins, right? I tell myself, This is not just for Jeopardy. I think this makes me a more well-rounded person who can maybe have a conversation with somebody that I wouldn’t have had before. Rosin: Did it ever lead to a genuine interest? Like, did you ever start listening to any of the operas that you learned about? Goins: I was listening to Carmen yesterday, actually. Goins: And I had not been an opera person before. Rosin: So you’ve talked about what you have to learn. How do you learn it? Memory, the memory palace, and memory tricks are a whole art form of their own. So now that you’ve gotten a sense of the landscape of things you have to know, how does one remember them? Goins: Kids’ reference books. So in addition to bounds of breadth that we’ve been discussing for the Jeopardy canon, it is also a very shallow canon. Jeopardy only asks one thing about most things. If Jeopardy asks about a European duchy, the European duchy is going to be Luxembourg. If it asks about an Iowa painter—I am sure there are great painters in Iowa who are not Grant Wood— Goins: —but if Jeopardy asks about it, the Iowa painter is gonna be Grant Wood. You kind of just have to learn one thing about everything. And what’s great for that are kids’ reference books. I’m talking about kids’ encyclopedias, kids’ atlases, all of those, like, What Is guides. So I checked out from my public library at the time What Is the Super Bowl? Goins: And I promise I know what the Super Bowl is, but I wanted that one thing beyond just football. Goins: These kids’ reference books—this is a trick that super-champion James Holzhauer also points to as having helped him—are great because they are really well shaped for the ethos of Jeopardy, of a survey of information that goes only so deep. And they’re colorfully illustrated. They are a joy to read. I grew up reading them, and when I went off to high school, college, whatever, I kind of reluctantly set them aside because I was like, Oh, well, I’m a serious adult now who needs to read books that don’t have pictures. And then when I got the call to go on Jeopardy, I was like, Ooh! For listeners, I’m rubbing my hands together gleefully right now. I was like, I get to read children’s reference books again. Goins: Because getting to learn a little bit about so much is just really, really delightful. Rosin: I was a little hung up on this kids’-reference-book thing, so I decided to put it to the test. I made Drew play a quick round of Jeopardy and had him explain how he knew or didn’t know the answers. And just like a real game of Jeopardy, Drew didn’t have access to the questions ahead of time. I swear. Rosin: Dennis Hopper won a Best Villain MTV Movie Award for menacing a bus in this film. Goins: That was a guess, but Jeopardy clues are written very economically. Very rarely is there a word in there that is not in some way pointing you to the answer. So I’ve, like—I had no idea Dennis Hopper was in that movie, which I have never seen, but I know Speed to be possibly the only movie that takes place almost entirely on a bus. After 3,388 performances and an eight-year run, this musical was no longer “the word” when it closed April 13, 1980. Goins: Another little clue in there. What is Grease? Goins and Rosin simultaneously: (Sings.) Grease is the word. Goins: See, I would not have known that just from the statistics of it, ’cause there are, you know, a lot of long-running musicals. But you have to know Jeopardy’s language too. This colorful cake got a boost in popularity from Steel Magnolias, where it was an armadillo-shaped groom’s cake. Goins: “This colorful cake.” So from there I’m thinking, Okay, what’s a cake with a color in the name? What is red-velvet cake? Rosin: Yes. I see how this is working. This is essentially: The Jeopardy mind would know to discount the irrelevant and very obscure—not necessarily obscure, but the more detailed information in here. Goins: Right. See, it’s great if you happen to have seen Steel Magnolias, which a lot of people have, but really well-written Jeopardy clues offer a backdoor into the answer as well. Rosin: Yeah. Yeah, ’cause “armadillo-shaped groom’s cake” is not gonna get you there. But “colorful cake,” there aren’t that many well-known cakes with a color. Goins: Yeah. So red velvet is a reasonable guess. Rosin: There’s no shortage of hacks and tricks you can learn to prepare for a Jeopardy game. But what can Jeopardy do for you? That’s after the break. Rosin: Okay, so I feel like we’ve covered the theory of Jeopardy. Now I wanna ask you about the reality. Rosin: What was your very first time on the show? Goins: My first time on the show was in June 2024. Jennings: Drew Goins is a journalist from Honolulu, Hawaii, and being from the Aloha State— Goins: And when you go on Jeopardy for the first time, they don’t put you up in a hotel. They don’t pay for travel or anything. You are paying for your own way. Even if you come in third, you get $2,000 as your prize. I think the thinking is: That covers your expenses, and you get to have this experience. But it is kind of isolating because you’re out in California, terra incognita, by yourself. And I showed up to the studio, and you go past the studio gates at Sony Picture Studios, and you’re ushered into this parking garage, and there are a circle of chairs and a metal detector, and that’s it. But then you go through that security screening, and then you go on to Stage 10, which is where the show is actually recorded, and all of a sudden, you open up the doors, and you’re greeted by a model podium and this wall of Emmys, and a hockey jersey that had been given to Trebek with his last name on it, and all of a sudden it feels so real. Goins: And you get just a chill. Oh, I’m giving myself chills right now describing it, which is how much of a dork I am. And I remember going into the greenroom for the first time. And in the months, years since I’ve played Jeopardy, I think of this greenroom as—this is gonna sound so hokey—but as one of my happy places. Because the people who are in there are your fellow contestants, who are just as nervous as you are and just as big of fans as learning inconsequentia. As the nervous energy wears off a little bit, people start warming up and they start sharing fun facts about things, and showing off their little trivia, maybe out of nervousness, maybe out of friendliness. A mixture of the two, possibly. And by an hour in, everybody’s just saying, “Oh, well, did you know this? Did you know this? Did you know that?” And everyone’s like, “Oh, yeah, of course, of course. Then what about this?” And it’s just this bacchanalian celebration of knowledge, and the knowledge is flowing left and right. It is so exciting to be in a room of people who aspire to what Jeopardy wants you to aspire to. Rosin: Okay, so you’re there in your first game. This is 2024, and how does it go? Rosin: What do you mean? You looked good losing, or? Goins: I certainly didn’t look good. I looked [like a] deer in the headlights. No, I played a really, really good game, and I lost narrowly. I was leading until the very end, when my competitor David Erb, he finds the final Daily Double and wagers really big. It’s a Civil War question. The answer is Lee’s Miserables, a play on Les Miserables, and he springs in front of me. And then he gets Final Jeopardy right. Jennings: So David Erb’s lead of $22,000, looking pretty good right now. Did he come up with Macbeth? He did. Well done. What did you wager, David? Goins: I get it wrong, adding insult to injury. Jennings: Drew Goins had an impressive second-place total of $16,800 today. Did he think of Macbeth? “Who is Titus Andronicus?” That’s even a bloodier play but unfortunately not correct. Goins: But I played well enough that they, a couple of months later, ring me up and say, “Would you like to come back for the Second Chance tournament?” So I feel surpassingly lucky to have had not just one opportunity to play this dream of mine, but two, when most people get zero bites at the apple. Jennings: Drew, welcome back. I hope you enjoyed your first appearance on Jeopardy. Goins: I ultimately win the Second Chance competition. So I go to the next one, and I finish second in that, but by some other odd twist of fate, one of the contestants who was supposed to go into the Tournament of Champions is not able to play. And so I take her spot, and then I make it to the semifinals of the Tournament of Champions, where I, sadly, once again, fall short, but was brought back again for this year’s Jeopardy Invitational Tournament, which, as I said earlier, “forever Jeopardy,” right? Who knows what’s next. Rosin: And what do Jeopardy champions do with their money? Goins: (Laughs.) Nothing! If you’re struggling to fall asleep any night, just call up a former Jeopardy champion and ask them what they did with their money, because I can guarantee you it is in a high-yield savings account, or it is in very safe index funds. I have a friend, Adriana, who won a whole bunch of money on the show, multiple times more than I won, and when she went back for a return appearance, she was asked how her life has changed since winning the money, and she said, “Oh, well, I’ve become a little more liberal with my boba budget.” Goins: I was like, “I should hope so. I really should hope so.” Rosin: So this is not like calling up lottery winners. Rosin: So I’ve heard people defend Jeopardy by saying, for example: “In an era of disinformation, we should respect this accumulation of facts.” Do you buy that theory? Goins: I think that some people who are real defenders of trivia and fans of it like to frame it as an act of resistance or defiance. But I think that asks trivia to do things that trivia is not well equipped to do. There’s this common response variable of being a good, educated person and enjoying Jeopardy, and that common response variable is loving knowledge. But I think we need to set trivia apart from that. What is so beautiful about trivia is that it really doesn’t matter. It is just knowledge purely for the sake of knowledge. I pick up these facts, and I get this little tickle of excitement when I see something that’s just sparkly or delightful, not because I know that I’m gonna use it, let alone use it to stop the creeping tide of authoritarianism in the United States, but because it’s this beautiful little jewel. It is good and delightful to know that the Tuskegee Airmen flew mostly P-51 Mustangs, but knowing that one little bit of trivia is not gonna stop anybody from removing the Tuskegee Airmen from a curriculum. That’s just not what trivia is for or capable of. Rosin: So then how did you start to think about knowledge or you in relation to knowledge? It’s not necessarily knowledge as liberation or as a path to liberation. It’s not necessarily knowledge as protest. It’s not necessarily knowledge as a thing you have to conquer and amass, and conquer and amass. So what is the value to you, or what has the value been to you over time? Goins: Preparing for and playing Jeopardy has really led me to see knowledge like I’d never seen it before, as this ocean out there. Goins: I think of my beach trip to Myrtle Beach, South Carolina, where Ken lost, and the next day when I was out on my raft that I had blown up with my own two lips, and just floating on top of this enormous body, knowing that I would never come close to experiencing all or even a small part of it, but delighting and just splashing right below the surface. And to me, that’s what trivia is like, where I can stand on the shore of this body and scoop out something that will bring me a little bit of joy. Rosin: Whew, Drew, I’m with you out there. Thank you so much for joining us today. Rosin: Listeners, if you want to try your hand at trivia, Drew has a new game on The Atlantic website. It’s called Rabbithole. A Stubbornly Naive Group of GOP Senators
2026-08-19

A Stubbornly Naive Group of GOP Senators

The Atlantic | 2026-08-19

Visit Original Website
This is an edition of The Atlantic Daily, a newsletter that guides you through the biggest stories of the day, helps you discover new ideas, and recommends the best in culture. Sign up for it here. For many observers of American politics, the past few years have been disillusioning, knocking down hopeful beliefs about what the people of the United States will support or tolerate. Even some of Donald Trump’s backers have become disaffected. Yet somehow, a small caucus of non-MAGA Republican senators has managed to remain preciously naive about the president and the administration he runs. No matter how many times Trump or his aides show who they are and how they operate, these senators seem flabbergasted and saddened. No case illustrates the ease with which they are fooled like the confirmation of Todd Blanche as attorney general. Blanche was confirmed last week on a 50–49 vote, but only after GOP Senators Thom Tillis and John Cornyn extracted a written assurance from Blanche that a $1.8 billion fund for payouts to Trump allies would not proceed. Senator Bill Cassidy also wavered, but said he was persuaded by the vow. (All three men will leave the Senate at the end of this term: Tillis is retiring, and Cornyn and Cassidy lost primaries to Trump-backed challengers.) This weekend, Blanche was interviewed on Meet the Press. Kristen Welker asked whether he could “pledge that the Justice Department will always act independently of the White House.” Blanche refused—likely an honest answer, given his demonstrated willingness to follow Trump’s lead, but one that should worry anyone who believes in the rule of law; witness Trump’s repeated demands for political prosecutions, irrespective of evidence. Yet Blanche insisted to Welker that Trump “will never ask me to do something unethical or illegal. He never has. He never will.” That might reassure someone whose political memory stretches back only as far as early August. But anyone old enough to remember the slush fund, or Blanche’s starring role in negotiating it (as laid out by Federal Judge Kathleen Williams in May), would know that Trump not only has asked Blanche to do something unethical, but that Blanche complied. In order to vote to confirm, Cornyn, Tillis, and Cassidy had to convince themselves first that Blanche’s conduct in that case is not indicative of his basic moral compass; and second that he would be more accountable once permanently confirmed by the Senate than he was when he still had to win over their votes. If Trump finds or attempts to find some way to resuscitate the slush fund—as some experts believe he might—many people will be dismayed, but only this trio of guileless senators will be shocked. Cassidy’s willingness to vote for a Trump yes-man is all the more puzzling because he voted to convict Trump in his 2021 impeachment trial, and because the president led the charge to defeat him in a primary. (The impeachment vote was Trump’s main reason for targeting him.) But Cassidy has a long record of credulity. As my colleague Nicholas Florko has reported, Cassidy—a physician—cast a deciding vote to confirm Secretary of Health and Human Services Robert F. Kennedy Jr., but only after the nominee made promises about preserving the existing vaccination regime, a pledge he promptly broke. On ABC’s The Week this past Sunday, Cassidy said “it’s pretty evident” that Kennedy’s vow was a lie. And on CNN’s State of the Union, he called the White House’s latest vaccine move, attempting to unbundle the measles, mumps, and rubella injection, “a crazy, stupid thing.” Senator John Barrasso of Wyoming, another doctor, voted for Kennedy and has since been upset about the administration’s anti-vaccine steps. Who could have foreseen that the prominent vaccine skeptic would undermine federal vaccination policy? Two other moderate Republicans, Susan Collins and Lisa Murkowski, voted against Blanche’s confirmation, issuing statements that noted the politicization of the Justice Department under his watch as acting attorney general. But they are not always so clear-eyed. Murkowski delivered one of 2025’s most unintentionally hilarious statements when she cast a crucial vote to pass Trump’s One Big Beautiful Bill Act through the Senate. “My sincere hope is that this is not the final product,” Murkowski said in a statement. “This bill needs more work across chambers and is not ready for the President’s desk. We need to work together to get this right.” Naturally, that didn’t happen; the mystery is why she thought the White House, or Mike Johnson’s House, would improve the bill once she’d surrendered her leverage. Collins, however, is the dean of the credulous caucus. The Mainer, who faces a tough reelection campaign this fall, is perpetually concerned about Trump-administration actions, but seldom ready to actually use her power. Collins is a rare pro-abortion-rights Republican who previously said she wouldn’t support any Supreme Court nominee who opposed Roe v. Wade. In 2018, she voted to confirm Justice Brett Kavanaugh, who said Roe was “settled as precedent.” When Kavanaugh was part of the majority that voted to overturn Roe in 2022, Collins lamented the ruling but now says she didn’t regret her vote for Kavanaugh and would do it again. More recently, Collins helped pass a huge appropriation for ICE with few strings attached, then expressed dismay at the use of the funds to buy “shock gloves” for agents. Even her vote against Blanche was symbolic, because he had enough votes to be confirmed without her. One could easily write these senators off as cynical, but if they’re acting for political gain, it’s not working well: Cassidy’s and Cornyn’s mild deviations from Trump led to the end of their careers; Tillis’s retirement helped him avoid what might have been a stiff primary challenge. Many of their concerns appear earnest. Perhaps they really just are that gullible, unable to adapt to a Trump age that is leaving them behind. In defending his Blanche vote, Cassidy shrugged off attacks: “I’ll be criticized for this vote. What’s new?” But voters might reasonably expect that their elected officials are savvier about the world than a babe in the woods. Here are three new stories from The Atlantic: Happiness, people will have you think, does not come from possessing things. It comes from love. Self-acceptance. Career satisfaction. Whatever. But here’s what everyone has failed to consider: the Ooni Koda 12-inch gas-powered outdoor pizza oven. Since I purchased mine a year ago, my at-home pizza game has hit levels that are inching toward pizzaiolo perfection. Like Da Vinci in front of a blank canvas, I now churn out perfectly burnished pies entirely from scratch—dough, sauce, caramelized onions, and all. By merely looking at a pie, I can tell you whether the cornicione is too puffy or just right, if the crust could use a bit more leoparding, and whether the dough should have spent another day in the fridge. I am now, in a word, pizza-pilled. But enlightenment is not without its consequences. Explore. Bumble’s “women message first” rule is dead—a change that reflects the hazy norms of dating today, Faith Hill argues. Go outside. Ensuring a daily dose of sunlight is good for your happiness, Arthur C. Brooks wrote last year. Rafaela Jinich contributed to this newsletter. When you buy a book using a link in this newsletter, we receive a commission. Thank you for supporting The Atlantic.

Are You Sure You Want a Car With a Giant Touch Screen?
2026-08-19

Are You Sure You Want a Car With a Giant Touch Screen?

The Atlantic | 2026-08-19

Visit Original Website
New drivers may never have to learn how to parallel park. At least not the old-fashioned way. Many new cars—a Ford Mustang Mach-E, a BMW sedan, a Tesla Cybertruck—will just maneuver into a spot for you with a click of a button. Even a decade-old Toyota Corolla has a backup camera and pedestrian detection. Cars that leave you to park all on your own are a dying breed: Any car manufactured after April 30, 2018, for sale in the United States is legally required to have a backup camera and, thus, a screen. After all, cars are just computers now. The screen (or really, screens) in today’s vehicles does a lot more than display rearview footage. It is essentially a tablet wedged into the dashboard that allows you to mirror your phone through Apple CarPlay, make a hands-free call via voice commands, Zoom into a work meeting, or even play a video game (not recommended). Mercedes-Benz debuted a 56-inch display in several models that it calls the “hyperscreen.” But the expectation—and in some cases, the requirement—that cars have an ever-growing set of electronic features comes with a cost. Just like laptops, cars depend on microprocessors and RAM, or random-access memory, to run all of their computations. “There’s just a baseline level of tech, and thus a baseline level of cost, required of every vehicle,” Karl Brauer, the executive analyst at iSeeCars, an automotive-research platform, told me. Technology is a major reason the average price of a new car in the U.S. has reached some $50,000, and that was before RAM became one of the most prized commodities in the world. AI companies are snatching up as much memory as possible for their data centers, causing a RAM shortage that has significantly raised the prices of phones, laptops, and just about any consumer-electronic device. Cars are next. The problem runs much deeper than a screen. Cars started morphing into computers in the 1970s, when new emissions standards made electronic systems necessary to manage ignition and fuel injection. There’s been a gradual software creep ever since: power windows, adaptive cruise control, antilock brakes. Regulators now rate cars based not just on how they do during a crash but also on how well their electronic safety systems avoid a crash. “We’ve evolved every single component of the vehicle to involve a semiconductor,” Ivan Drury, the director of insights at Edmunds, told me. More recently, Tesla in particular has played a big role in pushing the auto industry to stuff vehicles with even more digital technology. Whereas pistons and fuel lines make a gas-powered car move, electric vehicles are run by a computer that regulates their battery and motor. It’s no coincidence that Tesla helped popularize the giant in-car touch screen. Today’s new cars, electronic or otherwise, have driver-assistance features, receive over-the-air software updates, and can even load ChatGPT. BMW advertises the ability to have a distinct climate-control setting for every passenger and the option to install screens into seat backs, airplane-style. In most vehicles on American roads, all of this software is controlled by hundreds of separate electronic units throughout the car. Features such as antilock brakes and lane-detection sensors don’t need all that much processing power, and they can be operated by chips that might account for 5 to 10 percent of a vehicle’s materials cost, Sam Abuelsamid, an analyst at Telemetry and a former automotive engineer, told me. Again following the lead of EV start-ups such as Tesla and Rivian, legacy automakers are shifting from these ad hoc, tiny control units to one or two big computers that run all of the software in their vehicles—and, in turn, require much more RAM. These central computers allow for a streamlined supply chain, seamless software updates, and a faster internet connection, plus, as General Motors advertises, they will be ready for “future AI workloads.” You can’t really build a car that safely and reliably drives itself with a collection of puny computer chips. Such centralized computers need more advanced automotive microprocessors from Nvidia or Qualcomm and might eventually account for 20 percent or more of a vehicle’s cost, Abuelsamid said. That was before the AI-fueled memory shortage. In other words, cars are becoming more software-heavy and dependent on memory chips than ever, right when those chips are in high demand from tech companies willing to pay premiums that automakers cannot afford. Over the next year, the memory shortage—sometimes known as RAMageddon—will likely raise vehicle prices by a few percentage points, on average, Abuelsamid said. The relative amount would be smaller than the shocking double-digit jumps for gaming consoles and MacBooks but in some ways more significant: A 4 percent price hike for cars amounts to some $2,000 on average. Cars with those centralized computers will be affected the most, but no vehicle will be spared. “Even if you don’t need the high-end chips, you’re going to pay more even for the low-end chips just because of the supply constraint,” Abuelsamid said. On a recent earnings call, Ford’s chief financial officer said that the company had paid $1 billion in higher materials costs due to the memory shortage and inflation. GM and Volkswagen, too, have noted rising chip costs to investors. (Ford and GM did not respond to a request for comment. A spokesperson for Volkswagen told me that the company has “recognized an increased demand for memory chips, primarily driven by growing requirements in other industries,” and that in recent years, Volkswagen has taken measures to “mitigate supply risks.”) RAMageddon is poised to last for several years, but the consequences for car buyers may be permanent. Consider what happened during the pandemic, when supply-chain disruptions and rising demand for electronics produced a major chip shortage. Nearly every major car company had to slash production because they simply couldn’t procure enough chips, and shifted their focus to selling higher-end and higher-profit vehicles. Potential customers already willing to spend six figures on a car are much less likely to care about a 5 or 10 percent price hike, Drury said. Even now, car companies are continuing to focus on selling more profitable models. Since the pandemic, the average price of a new vehicle has jumped $11,000. The AI-fueled chip crisis could play out more severely. The average price of a new car could, before long, jump to $60,000 and beyond. These rising costs are making cars even more similar to computers and all of the software they run: Perhaps in an effort to mitigate higher prices, some automakers are also introducing in-car advertisements and putting certain features, such as heated seats, behind a paywall. As cars have morphed into computers, the inevitable next step is for automakers to behave like modern tech companies. Most Americans cannot afford a $60,000 new car, let alone a $50,000 one. When new-vehicle prices soar, the used-car market follows. That has been one durable consequence of the pandemic’s chip crisis: The average price of a three-year-old used car is up $9,000, or nearly 40 percent, since before the pandemic. To get the same prices, Americans already have to buy much older cars that have far more miles on them. If automakers continue to shift their focus to designing cars with more software, more screens, more self-driving features, “that increasingly prices people even out of the used-vehicle market,” Abuelsamid said. It’s one thing for the AI industry’s appetite for memory chips to raise the price of an Xbox, or even a new laptop. But the stakes are much more consequential when it becomes harder and more expensive to get groceries, pick the kids up from school, and go to work.
The Atlantic
Why the Closing of Harvard’s Writing Center Matters
2026-08-19

Why the Closing of Harvard’s Writing Center Matters

The Atlantic | 2026-08-19

Visit Original Website
It is not every day that the laying off of a university bureaucrat generates a torrent of criticism from students, faculty, and alumni. But Jane Rosenzweig was not just any university bureaucrat, and she was not presiding over just any administrative fiefdom. The dismantling of Harvard University’s writing center, and the laying off of Rosenzweig, its director—both announced last month—has been met with outcry shading into outrage. It has produced a minor literature of student-newspaper coverage, disapproving social-media posts, and angry emails. An open letter from current and former writing-center tutors protesting the decision had more than 100 signatories. The passionate response is partly a testament to Rosenzweig herself, who was a beloved figure on campus. Among the flood of comments from former writing-center employees were a number that described her as the best boss they’d ever had. The backlash is also about what Rosenzweig represents: She has been a champion of human writing in the age of AI, through both her work at the center—where undergraduate students received coaching on how to revise and refine papers for their classes—and her essays on Substack. “Our students are not products to be moved down a frictionless assembly line,” she wrote in 2024, “and the hard work of reading, writing, and thinking is not a problem to be solved.” Rosenzweig, who worked as an editor at The Atlantic from 1994 to 1997, has become a kind of synecdoche for the very idea of taking writing and the liberal arts seriously at Harvard in the post-ChatGPT world. Brandon Kingdollar, a former tutor who graduated in 2024, wrote in a comment on the open letter that her layoff “sends a clear message to today’s students that nurturing their abilities to write and think for themselves is no longer a priority.” Harvard’s leadership, he added, seems to be saying that “it is perfectly acceptable” for students to “outsource these important skills to AI agents.” A Harvard faculty member who requested anonymity so that he could speak openly told me he worried that the elimination of the center could be seen as part of a broader trend of “real boosterism” of AI on campus—one that students feel acutely. “There is an impression among students that they’re being told by people in positions of authority that really getting good at using AI is essential to what college is going to be for them and for their future career success,” he said. Read: ChatGPT doesn’t have to ruin college Harvard’s undergraduate college has held AI training sessions and given students access to premium chatbots including Google’s Gemini, OpenAI’s ChatGPT Edu, and Anthropic’s Claude. The university also maintains a webpage directed at faculty members titled “Teach With Generative AI”—oddly phrased as a command—that lays out resources for “leveraging LLM features” and boasts about Harvard’s record of embracing “new technology across our classrooms, residential and virtual.” Students and faculty might have good reason to use AI products in certain educational contexts. But the university generally leaves the policing of these “resources” up to individual faculty members, even as AI use has become widespread among students. A survey conducted this year by The Harvard Crimson found that the average student used AI to complete 34.5 percent of their homework, and that more than one in 10 students used AI to complete 70 to 100 percent of their work. Harvard’s dean of undergraduate education, Amanda Claybaugh, told me that “we must teach students what they need to know about AI, while also ensuring that AI doesn't interfere with everything else.” She also said that concerns about closing the writing center are “largely a problem of terminology.” She went on, “Consolidating the writing center sounds alarming until you understand that the ‘writing center’ was merely a sub-unit of the Harvard Writing Program, which will continue to oversee our first-year writing courses.” The school still employs many experts on writing pedagogy, she said, and Harvard’s writing program will make sure that students receive “the best, most up-to-date, guidance about writing in an age of AI.” Some in the Harvard community see the demise of the center as simply a regrettable but necessary concession to financial conditions. The school has a reported $365 million budget shortfall and has been making a number of changes to address it. One tenured professor in the humanities, who requested anonymity because of how controversial the decision has become, told me that the school has had to do “a couple of rounds of very painful cuts.” The professor said that he believes that eliminating the writing center could also help address a well-documented problem of administrative bloat. Harvard’s administrative staff has expanded at several times the rate of faculty in recent years. “Everyone thinks, in the abstract, there should be belt tightening,” the professor said. “But whenever a concrete person is let go, everyone screams.” Still, the fact that some administrators should be laid off and some academic centers should be closed does not make it wise to lay off this administrator and to close this center. When an institution begins subjecting something so fundamental to a college education as writing to the cold logic of spreadsheets and bureaucratic streamlining, it has conceded quite a lot already. This concession may ultimately open the door for administrators of the future to argue that writing instruction should be turned over to robots for the sake of savings and efficiency. “Harvard must make difficult budgetary decisions sometimes,” Mary D. Lewis, a French-history professor, wrote in an email sent to senior administrators. “But the writing center was not an expensive enterprise, and it will likely cost us more down the road to undo the damage done.” Some of that potential damage will be contained to Harvard. Instructors may have less support in their efforts to defend traditional forms of learning from technological change. Students may have less incentive to do the challenging work of original writing instead of turning to ChatGPT. But the consequences of the decision to close the writing center may be felt far beyond the campus in Cambridge. Last year, I traveled to Montana State University for a week as a writer-in-residence. There, I met with students and faculty to talk about journalism and creative nonfiction. The highlight of the trip was a Q&A session with the university’s writing-center tutors. I left with an ultramarine MSU Writing Center T-shirt and an overwhelming admiration for the students and staff who were doing their best, with limited resources and watery office coffee, to make a case for going to the center rather than using the bots. From the September 2026 issue: Why I quit the tenure track The tutors I spoke with at MSU said that they try to model writing as a creative, collaborative activity, one that happens between a scholar and a community rather than between an individual and his or her screen. They meet with students one-on-one to brainstorm ideas, read essays out loud, and critique their personal styles. When I heard the news about Harvard’s writing center, my first thought was not about the students and instructors there, but about the ones back in Montana. College and university bureaucracies tend to have a strong mimetic streak. Institutions with lower academic rankings often emulate the initiatives and priorities of their higher-ranked peers. Harvard is the apex predator in America’s higher-ed ecosystem, and it has substantial power to influence what counts as essential to a good education. The decisions that Harvard makes create a permission structure for other institutions to follow suit—after all, if one of the most venerable schools in the country doesn’t need a writing center in order to be “elite,” why would a public university, or a regional liberal-arts college, or a community college need one? Why does Montana State need one? Administrators, boards of trustees, and state legislatures will almost certainly be asking these questions. Harvard has made it easier for them to decide that original writing—and, by extension, original thought—isn’t worth the cost.

The ‘Resistance’ Is Over
2026-08-19

The ‘Resistance’ Is Over

The Atlantic | 2026-08-19

Visit Original Website
Is Donald Trump over? Can we all now relax and move on to the next thing? That’s the message from a significant portion of Democratic primary voters. Just as Trump is bored with Iran and the economy and wants to play with monuments and gilding, so too are many Democratic primary voters in Florida and elsewhere bored with Trump and eager to play factional politics instead. The political statistician Nate Silver summed up the mood on X today: “It’s kind of been a rough set of primaries for Heroes of the Resistance 1.0.” Silver then listed stalwart anti-Trumpers who have lost their contests in 2026 and concluded, “People want to move on.” America is a fast-moving, dynamic country. It’s tough for Americans to stay focused on a single thing, even if that single thing is the threat to American democracy. As the celebrity spokesmodel Heidi Klum tells the audience on Project Runway: “In fashion, one day you’re in, and the next day, you’re out.” This law also apparently applies to Democratic candidates who took serious risks to challenge Trump. The resistance, it seems, is out. The far left of the Democratic Party has never been as energized as the party’s center about Trump and democracy. A 2017 analysis in The Washington Post suggested that between 6 and 12 percent of people who voted for Bernie Sanders in the 2016 Democratic primaries voted for Trump in the general election. With many careful caveats, the analysis observed that these voters exceeded Trump’s margin of victory. In 2024, likewise, some leaders of the activist left noisily defected from the Harris-Walz ticket. For many of them, opposing Israel’s war against Hamas in Gaza mattered more than stopping a Trump presidency at home. The effect of this mobilization on the left is hard to measure, but The Nation was quick to insist that the Biden administration’s Gaza policy likely cost the party Michigan, because many Arabs and Muslims swung toward Trump. The article did not seriously consider whether culture politics, such as Kamala Harris’s support for transgender rights, influenced socially conservative Arab and Muslim voters, even though the city of Dearborn has long been a locus of anti-gay grassroots politics in the state. The claim that Harris lost to Trump because of Gaza may well be activist self-medication, but this kind of story about the election is telling. This year, the dulling of anti-Trump impetus on the left is even more visible and consequential. Again and again, primary voters have made choices that subordinate general-election concerns to factional power-seeking. Democratic leaders—the dreaded “Establishment”—have promoted highly plausible candidates for statewide office in North Carolina, Texas, and other suddenly competitive states. But the insurgent left has again and again upset leadership plans with high-risk choices to serve its own factional agenda, putting at risk once-probable Senate victories in Maine, Michigan, and some House seats too. Donald Trump is not that important. His attacks on the rule of law, on free and peaceful elections, on U.S. alliances, on trade and markets—those are all mere symptoms of a disease, not the disease itself. Political energy should be focused on treating the deeper problems of which Trump is a mere passing expression, even at the risk of losing the Senate in 2026 and forfeiting the presidency in 2028. “Winning” with Chuck Schumer–approved candidates is no win at all. Better to risk losing than to “win” on Schumer’s terms. Jonathan Chait: There’s nothing democratic about these socialists The Democratic left is making a complicated—and quite ironic—internal gamble here. The faction’s efforts to hijack the larger party to their own unpopular causes will work only if they can convince Democratic voters that Trump is not the most important and urgent problem facing Americans. Yet the only reason Democratic centrists would coalesce around the party’s leftist candidates in November is if they believe in the absolute urgency of stopping Trump. Many of the far-left candidates are aggressively obnoxious from a centrist’s point of view. Centrist Democrats are not socialists, not revolutionaries, and not disposed to blame Zionist conspiracies for every trouble in American life, including police brutality and urban gentrification. Why on earth would centrist Democrats in Michigan vote for a radical leftist who calls most Democrats hypocrites when the other choice on the ballot is a Republican campaigning on reducing housing costs by simplifying the government-approval process? In Florida last night, Democrats rejected Alex Vindman, the former national-security official who at immense personal and career risk blew the whistle on Trump’s 2019 plot to blackmail Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky into fabricating campaign dirt against Joe Biden. Voters chose instead Angie Nixon, who campaigned as an explicit “socialist” in a state that is home to hundreds of thousands of Cuban American and Venezuelan American refugees from socialism, and who ran alongside anti-Israel figures such as Oliver Larkin and Rashida Tlaib in a state with the nation’s third-biggest Jewish population. From a Democratic-centrist point of view, if Trump’s attacks on the rule of law are not a supreme and imperative threat, then a Republican Senate majority led by John Thune is an endurable nuisance. Far-left candidates have a chance in the general election only if Democrats believe that Trump is the supreme and imperative threat. The remains of the suddenly unfashionable Democratic resistance can only hope that Democrats in Texas, North Carolina, Michigan, and elsewhere will rise above the factional politics and remember in November what, and who, remains the most pressing danger to the nation’s law and democracy.

When companies throw around terms like "cities and towns" for drone logistics, they are usually talking about sprawling suburban developments with neat, wide-open lawns, not dense urban cores. Even with this big rollout targeting nearly 500 communities, the operational reality dictates where these things can actually land: Amazon isn't building 500 new drone stations; they are counting every municipality touched by a regional network. A single facility covers a massive swath of territory (roughly 175 square miles), which inevitably zeroes in on the suburbs and outer rings of places like Atlanta, Chicago, and Dallas rather than downtown high-rises. The Landing Zone Problem: For a drone to safely drop a package (even a modest 5-pound shoebox-sized item), it needs a clear, unobstructed patch of ground free of overhead power lines, tight balconies, narrow alleyways, and inquisitive pets. Trump Can’t Stop, Won’t Stop (Undermining the 2026 Elections)
2026-08-20

Trump Can’t Stop, Won’t Stop (Undermining the 2026 Elections)

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-08-20

Visit Original Website
This is Executive Dysfunction, a newsletter that highlights one under-the-radar story about how Trump is changing the law—or how the law is pushing back—and keeps you posted on the latest from Slate’s Jurisprudence team. Click here to receive it in your inbox each week. President Donald Trump’s attempt to nationalize America’s voting system suffered another loss in court this week, just the latest in a lengthy series of defeats over Trump’s blatantly unlawful executive orders around voting. This time was a bit more dramatic, though, with a judge bluntly calling out the Justice Department for using a lawsuit against Minnesota as a “fishing expedition” to help it find a violation of federal law. U.S. District Court Judge Katherine Menendez dismissed the case, ultimately finding that Congress never authorized the attorney general to access voting records and documents that state officials create themselves. These continued losses won’t end the story, though. Newly confirmed Attorney General Todd Blanche announced this week that he’s considering asking the Supreme Court to intervene in the dozens of lawsuits his DOJ has filed against states that, like Minnesota, refused to hand over their voter rolls. Trump and Blanche are attempting to bully states into submission over “election integrity,” but it’s obvious what the real endgame is: Complete control over U.S. elections. All of this began about four months ago, when Trump signed an executive order targeting mail voting. It directed Homeland Security to create a national list of all U.S. citizens over the age of 18 who would be eligible to vote, and then that list would be sent to all 50 states. Each state would then have to send the United States Postal Service a separate list of all of its eligible voters—presumably it would need to match DHS’ list—so when election time rolled around, USPS could refuse a mail ballot from someone not on a state’s shared voter list. Immediately upon Trump signing this EO, it was obvious to many that it would be found unconstitutional by the courts. Regardless of that fact, the Trump administration pushed forward, now having established legal footing to force states to work with the federal government and, more importantly, fueling a massive step toward nationalizing America’s elections. Most states did not fall for this scheme and refused to create or send any voter lists to the federal government, prompting the DOJ to sue 30 states and D.C. for noncompliance. So far, most of these lawsuits have been dismissed, while 16 predominantly Republican states agreed to comply with Trump’s EO. The case in Menendez’s courtroom was a bit different. Initially, Minnesota did share some information when the DOJ asked, mostly relating to maintenance of its voter rolls, which is congressionally mandated. However, the state refused to send anything more detailed, as it includes sensitive identifying information like Social Security numbers. However, the DOJ was unsatisfied and continued to push for full access to Minnesota’s voter rolls, which eventually culminated in the lawsuit. That effort has effectively been put to bed, as Menendez concluded that Congress never intended to allow the AG to access voter records and papers that state election officials create. The AG can only access voter records that come into the possession of state election officials, a critical distinction which Menendez says the DOJ intentionally misconstrued. “The unredacted statewide voter registration list the government demanded from the state defendants in this case is not a record or paper that comes into Minnesota election officials’ possession,” she wrote. Menendez also found the DOJ’s argument that Minnesota could be violating federal election law and therefore it must hand over its voter rolls to be disingenuous. “The government suggests it is permitted to use a civil action as a ‘fishing expedition,’ ” she wrote. “But an ordinary litigant isn’t allowed to seek discovery first and allege a claim later, and the federal government has no special privilege to do so.” Thanks to Menendez, the DOJ’s graveyard of dismissed election-related lawsuits just grew an inch. But this administration is not one to cut its losses and run. Last month, the Trump administration asked the Supreme Court on an emergency basis to allow it to fully implement the presidential executive order that would order the USPS to refuse to mail ballots to voters if they didn’t appear on a Trump-approved list. Back in June, after considering a lawsuit brought by 23 states and D.C., U.S. District Judge Indira Talwani issued an injunction that prevented the federal government from implementing that EO. A divided appeals court also turned down the Trump administration’s request to intervene, because such a drastic change to U.S. mail-voting processes so close to the midterm elections “would sow confusion and threaten disenfranchisement of many eligible voters.” The Trump administration’s request of SCOTUS flies in the face of a decades-old doctrine known as the Purcell principle, coined by Slate contributor Richard L. Hasen, which demands that any significant election-related changes not be made close to an election, because it risks voter confusion and administrative chaos, as Donathan L. Brown, a civil rights scholar and associate professor at Northeastern University, explained recently in Slate. The states responded to the lawsuit earlier this month, and now we wait to see if the justices decide to weigh in. Meanwhile, the Trump administration is actively working other angles, demanding the federal government be given access to voting equipment. Colorado and Missouri have faced these requests, and Georgia was the scene of a full-on FBI raid earlier this year, in which federal officials seized more than 600 boxes of voter data. When you consider that there are less than three months left until November’s consequential midterm elections, plus the president is earning a 61 percent disapproval rating, and 10 of his endorsed candidates for statewide office or Congress have lost their primaries so far, it’s clear the Trump administration has reason to be panicking. Unfortunately for Americans, that means the lawless attacks on our election apparatus may be about to hit an inflection point. We hope you learned a thing or two from this edition of Executive Dysfunction, and if you enjoyed reading it, please consider supporting our legal journalism by becoming a Slate Plus member! This week’s Amicus features a special crossover with Slate podcast Slow Burn! Listeners can dive into Episode 1 of the latest season of Slow Burn: Becoming Justice Gorsuch, which explores the early years of President Donald Trump’s first Supreme Court appointee. It’s a must-listen to understand how the current 6–3 Roberts court came to be and where it’s headed. The Amicus bonus episode revisits a 2021 conversation among Dahlia Lithwick, Judge Edward M. Chen, and Don Tamaki, members of the legal team that overturned Korematsu v. United States. The 1994 case about the internment of Japanese American citizens is still relevant today as the Supreme Court has failed to fully contend with its legacy. As Democrats prepare to flip control of the House of Representatives, one of the first agenda items will likely be considering another impeachment of the president. Frank Bowman, law professor and former federal and state prosecutor, writes in Slate about how lawmakers should approach this mighty task. Mark Joseph Stern and Dahlia Lithwick answer some of Amicus listeners’ burning questions. In a new Dear (Juris)Prudence column, the duo answer a question about whether President Trump’s massive accumulation of personal wealth while occupying the White House constitutes an emolument. Mark also gives his take on how a growing number of Supreme Court opinions are seemingly founded on baseless notions about the law and history. Every few weeks, Americans are peppered with headlines about how immigration officers are killing immigrants, deploying disturbing acts of violence, and generally behaving with reckless disregard for the law. I unpack how this all comes down to one factor: Money, and lots of it. Thank you for reading Executive Dysfunction!
