All Michigan Democrats Have to Do to Win Is to Find Peace Over the Middle East
2026-08-20

All Michigan Democrats Have to Do to Win Is to Find Peace Over the Middle East

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-08-20

Visit Original Website
Sign up for the Slatest to get the most insightful analysis, criticism, and advice out there, delivered to your inbox daily. Jeremy Moss and Abdul El-Sayed are young, diverse progressive millennials in Michigan who are on the ballot for federal office in November. But Moss says he’s not yet ready to endorse his party’s nominee for Senate. Moss is the Democratic nominee for Congress in a safe suburban Detroit seat. He is eager to come to Washington with Democratic majorities in both chambers of Congress. “We need to ensure that Trump’s cruelty and chaos is stopped, and specifically also to ensure that Trump isn’t capable of selecting younger and more conservative and extreme judges who will serve a lifetime,” the 40-year-old Moss told Slate. That desire for a majority—and for a check on Donald Trump—is not all that Moss shares with El-Sayed, the Democratic Senate nominee in Michigan. “Abdul and I have agreed on some really big issues. There’s no daylight between us on abolishing ICE, attacking billionaires, and having a bolder, tougher, stronger Democratic Party with new leadership,” said Moss. Like El-Sayed, Moss is a progressive millennial who has long been a rising star in Michigan Democratic politics. He was the first openly gay state senator in Michigan history and has risen into legislative leadership during his decade of service in Lansing. In many ways, he even sounds like El-Sayed in his view of politics. In Moss’ opinion, “The Democratic Party has to be bolder, tougher, and stronger. The line I use on the campaign trail all the time that always gets a rise out of people is ‘We can no longer be a party that shows up to the knife fight carrying a pie chart.’ ” However, Moss tells Slate, he is not yet ready to back El-Sayed himself in the U.S. Senate race. That’s true even as El-Sayed runs against Republican Mike Rogers, whom Moss describes as “an election denier, somebody who’s going to keep Trump’s ICE running and rubber-stamp his extreme judicial nominees.” But Moss is Jewish, is running to represent a district with a strong Jewish community, and is a supporter of Israel. His backing of a two-state solution and disdain for Benjamin Netanyahu had traditionally been Democratic orthodoxy. However, that’s no longer the case. In contrast, El-Sayed is a virulent critic of Israel. Earlier this summer, he told CNN: “Every definition of a Jewish state ends up in some articulation of illiberal values, every single one.” And he said that if you’re “a Democrat and you believe in human rights,” the only reason one could support Israel is campaign contributions. The candidate, who is Muslim, also said, “My commitment to Jewish safety is the same as the commitment I’ve got to my own kids,” but to many, his criticism of Israel has veered into antisemitic tropes. That’s particularly true after he campaigned with Hasan Piker, who has made antisemitic remarks and praised Hamas. The result has left many in Michigan’s Jewish community wary of El-Sayed and looking for outreach from him and his campaign to assuage their concerns. “I think we both recognize it’s going to take Jeremy Moss voters and Abdul El-Sayed voters to vote for the same ticket to beat Mike Rogers and Donald Trump and MAGA extremism, and as we saw on Tuesday night’s results, there is a lot of overlap there,” Moss tells Slate. “But we’re going to sit down, and Abdul’s going to have to demonstrate some serious outreach to Oakland County’s Jewish community.” As the political season turns from primaries to the general election, much has been made of the tension between centrist and progressive candidates. But some of those divides are more easily healed than others. Democratic differences over healthcare can be brushed aside by all parties involved, agreeing that their positions are superior to the GOP approach. Same with questions about policing or immigration or a host of other issues. But it remains to be seen if the divide in the conflict between Israel and the Palestinians can be bridged when a growing number of Democrats no longer support Israel’s existence as a Jewish state—and when, at times, anti-Zionist rhetoric has spilled over into antisemitic rhetoric. Jewish Americans have long been one of the most consistently pro-Democratic demographics in the country. The last time a plurality of American Jews did not support the Democratic nominee for president was in 1920. However, many in the party have in recent years become increasingly hostile toward Israel, whose existence is supported by a vast majority of American Jews. At the same time, a growing Muslim American population has viewed strident condemnation of Israel in the aftermath of the Oct. 7 terrorist attack, if not opposition to its very existence, as a threshold issue. It also has faced plenty of discrimination, with some Republican lawmakers insisting that Muslims should not be allowed to hold office in the United States. This tension is particularly pointed in metro Detroit, which has the largest Arab American population in the country, as well as a significant Jewish population. And the issue has become more salient since the Oct. 7 Hamas attack on Israel and the ensuing Israeli military campaign in Gaza. Rogers’ campaign has already been actively reaching out to Michigan Jews concerned about El-Sayed’s rhetoric and has found some Democrats willing to back him. There are over 100,000 Jews in Michigan, and Rogers lost his 2024 Senate race to Democrat Elissa Slotkin by less than 20,000 votes. Moss’ primary win came only months after his congressional district witnessed a terrorist attack on a synagogue where an armed Hezbollah-inspired attacker drove a car rigged with explosives into the building, which had a preschool in session at the time. A security guard shot the attacker before any children were harmed. The attack still lingers in metro Detroit’s close-knit Jewish community, which is centered on Moss’ congressional district. As he recalled, growing up he learned about antisemitism “in history books, black-and-white photos, and film reels.” But the attack on Temple Israel was in “real color … it wasn’t in Germany or Poland, but it was in West Bloomfield, where terrorists had the means and motive to kill 100 children.” In Moss’ conversation with Slate only a few days after his early August primary victory, it was clear the Michigan Democrat wanted to support the Democratic nominee for Senate, but he just wasn’t there yet. “There are areas of disagreement, and there’s still a lot of Jewish pain in this district, especially after the Temple Israel attack here, and I’ve talked to Abdul about this over the last year in real time, and we’ve had an ongoing dialogue.” The conflict faced by Moss is not unusual. He noted that at a time when there is hostile language on the left toward American Jews and Israel, “we have to make sure that the liberal Jewish community is not politically homeless.” He stated that his victory “really demonstrates that there is a lane for that community here in our party, and you know I’m representing that, as well as representing a lot of other big Democratic issues that this community voted for, that this district voted for.” Moss said he has been a “leader on some of these consequential fights in Michigan, from gun safety to voting rights—lent me a lot of credibility as somebody who took on progressive issues.” He added: “And so to be able to stand up and say ‘Hey, it is not incompatible to be a Jewish leader and a progressive leader’ is something that clearly voters agreed with and embraced.” These tensions spilled over not just into the Senate race, in which El-Sayed narrowly edged out his pro-Israel Democratic opponent Rep. Haley Stevens, but into Moss’ successful primary campaign. His opponents in his primary flirted with antisemitic tropes attacking him, claiming that he put “Israel first” and repeatedly trying to associate him with AIPAC, the lobby group that supports the U.S.–Israel alliance, despite the fact that the group did not back him. As Moss told Jewish Insider at the time, those attacks “touch many people’s ears as if it’s trafficking antisemitism.” Even after winning his primary, disinformation about Moss that delved into antisemitism has circulated widely. Remarkably for the candidate, his experience as an activist leading the charge for LGBTQ+ rights—fighting for antidiscrimination protections at a time even before the Obergefell decision, which legalized gay marriage—has prepared him for life as a Jewish elected official in these fraught times. He noted that the best antidote to prejudice is “getting elected and representing a community and dispelling myths and dispelling conspiracies and dispelling lies in order to show up as your true, authentic self and better explain what a community is about.” He added: “And so I’ve done that, being the only LGBTQ member of the Michigan Senate, and I’ve done that being the only Jewish member of the Michigan Senate.” The result, he said, has helped make inroads with colleagues who “see quick clips on TikTok about what’s going on thousands of miles away, and not letting that be the only thing that dictates their opinions and their values and their policies, and digging a little bit deeper to explain the nuances of an incredibly complicated conflict.” For Moss, outreach by El-Sayed is important not just because of the high political stakes involved and the urgent need he felt to ensure that Democrats could hold a swing Senate seat. As a lifelong Michigander who values the state’s diversity, he doesn’t want to see divisions grow. “For us to figure out the path forward, and not even in a political sense, not even in the context of an election, in order for a very vibrant Jewish community and a very vibrant Arab American community to be able to continue to live here as neighbors, there has to be figuring out the path forward to continue to come together,” he said. As Moss noted, “We don’t always agree, and we have a different lens on this conflict, but we are all trying to make sure that the conflict thousands of miles away does not come here at home … We’re all still here together as neighbors, and we have to make sure that these two communities can continue to live side by side here as neighbors.” Moss and El-Sayed have been in touch. Their divide is far more soluble than that of the underlying conflict in the Middle East, but bridging it still remains a work in progress. Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. A Streamer Predicted He Could Kill a Black Person and Get Away With It. Thousands Watched. When He Shot Someone, the Real Story Began.
2026-08-20

A Streamer Predicted He Could Kill a Black Person and Get Away With It. Thousands Watched. When He Shot Someone, the Real Story Began.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-08-20

Visit Original Website
Joshua Fox put it to me plainly: “I just don’t wanna be known as the Black guy Chud the Builder shot.” His point is easy to empathize with. It’s also hard to honor, considering the nature of my work. The reason I was talking to him was because he’d been shot by Chud the Builder. But, at a time when Fox is the subject of an online demonization campaign by Chud’s defenders, I wanted to hear his side of the story. That, as you’ll learn, was difficult to do. But before I spoke with Fox—the victim and protagonist in this terrifying saga who is just now breaking his silence—I spent months tracking his would-be shooter online: a depraved, attention-seeking opportunist who coveted the infamy of killing a Black man. Over the past two years, Dalton Levi Eatherly, a 29-year-old contractor turned ragebait streamer, concocted a digital persona known as “Chud the Builder,” now notorious in far-right circles for producing N-word outrage-porn rooted in crude free-speech absolutism that contends that if Black people can say the N-word, so can we, because this is America, goddamnit! Chud’s money-making blueprint involved filming himself hunting down Black strangers on the streets of Tennessee towns while yelling racist phrases like “Chimping out” to provoke a reaction he could monetize. Often duded up in a cowboy hat and suspenders, Eatherly had a knack for marketing and proved to be an exceptional conman. Using his handsome hillbilly looks and intimate understanding of Black vernacular, he would often coax his targets with disarming banter before dropping the N-bombs. Once he got a rise out of his subject, Chud would flash his gun, coyly grin, and dare them to cower or physically respond, while filming in a red state with proactive “stand your ground” laws and a blood-soaked history of racial violence and subjugation. This history of lynching and bondage and rape and second-class citizenship has since given rise to a pernicious albeit far more subtle prejudice, a disdain that failed to collapse despite Appomattox and Brown v. Board and the “post-racial” Obama era. Eatherly understood this. His entire charade was a dangerous double-bind ambush, engineered to emasculate or incite violence. And as a source of income, it was also one of the newest iterations of the American dream, reliant on one of our oldest tropes. All of which is disturbing enough, if not surprising. But that is not all Eatherly did. We are here because Eatherly went well beyond brandishing a loaded epithet. We are here because one spring day in May, Joshua Fox, a 31-year-old Black disabled veteran with post-traumatic stress disorder and a checkered past, stood up and stress-tested Eatherly’s business model. Chud’s grievance-fueled “work” was premised on the notion of superiority. But under his facade of online success was a life in disarray: He’d lost his construction job. He’d tarnished his reputation in his hometown. He was living in an overgrown and ramshackle double-wide trailer (from which he’s now allegedly being evicted), and was busy panhandling on GiveSendGo, the Christian-right knockoff of GoFundMe, while out on two separate bonds for harassment and theft in two different jurisdictions. Then, at 9 a.m. on May 13, Eatherly was scheduled to appear before Judge H. Reid Poland III’s General Sessions Court in the Montgomery County courthouse in Clarksville, Tennessee. He was being summoned because he was being sued by Midland Credit Management for $3,466.63 in defaulted credit card debt. But like so many high-born Anglo-Saxons before him, Eatherly chose to no-show to court. Accordingly, Judge Poland entered a default judgment into the record, which gave Midland Credit Management the right to tread on his bank account and garnish his wages. Nevertheless, for reasons still not understood, four hours later Eatherly did make an appearance at the courthouse. Well, outside of the courthouse: At roughly 1:15 p.m., dressed in a blazer and Oxford shirt, with selfie stick in hand and a gun in his jacket pocket, Eatherly strolled into the municipal plaza and pinpointed the lone Black couple sitting on the concrete planters. Shaniqua and Joshua Fox were there for a 1:30 p.m. child custody hearing. Chud was there to instigate trouble. What happened next is already being fought over on the internet and soon will be adjudicated in court. What is clear from the audio-less courthouse CCTV footage that captured the event is that Eatherly approached the couple. Words were exchanged between unarmed Joshua Fox and armed Dalton Eatherly. Eatherly walked away. Then Fox got up, approached Eatherly and made gestures toward him. Eatherly switched the selfie stick to his left hand and put his right hand in his jacket pocket. Fox walked away and then came back again. Then, according to both the lead investigator in the case, Detective Michael Weber, and the text of the arrest warrant, Eatherly “turned his body in a bladed stance” while reaching for his gun. “Thereafter,” the warrant states, “a physical altercation ensued.” Fox punched Eatherly, and the men wrestled to the ground. Seven shots were fired in rapid succession. Eatherly shot himself in the arm and shot Fox five times at point-blank range. Fox was airlifted to Vanderbilt Hospital—and ultimately survived. Two days later, Judge Poland the III and Dalton Eatherly the Chud would get another chance to meet. Eatherly shuffled into Poland’s courtroom on May 15 in a dark-blue jumpsuit “reserved for high-risk inmates,” facing four charges, all far different from failing to pay back a loan: attempted first degree murder, employing a firearm during a dangerous felony, aggravated assault, and reckless endangerment. Due to the gravity of the offenses, the location where they were perpetrated, and the fact that he was already out on two separate bonds, Poland initially set his new bond at $1.25 million. (It would later be reduced to a still-formidable $1 million.) Upon hearing Poland’s order, Eatherly closed his eyes in a look of utter devastation. Just as his digital persona reached its apex, his real-world life was locked in a cage. Once I got word of the shooting, and learned Joshua Fox was alive and Dalton Eatherly was at the mercy of the county jail, I got to work. As a freelance writer based in the South, I immediately realized I needed to become obsessed with the details of this case and start reporting. I’d been following Eatherly’s vile commentary on the cesspool that is X, formerly known as Twitter, for longer than I care to admit, thanks in part to Elon Musk’s warped hard-right algorithms. Chud’s MO was pitiful and enraging—a surefire way to trigger the primordial parts of one’s brain. But from a purely free-market, late-stage-capitalist perspective, it was also wickedly effective. Like many others, I too had clicked. Took the bait. And became a node in Chud’s attention-farming funding scheme. Mainly because I was appalled. After all, the Ku Klux Klan needed to wear hoods to mask their identities a century ago. Now, Eatherly’s ilk gloats about verbally terrorizing American citizens going about their day-to-day lives on American streets and beams this content around the globe for money. Either you rallied behind Chud or you rooted for his demise; either way, he got paid.It was a sick new business model and digital pathogen. As his footprint grew, he became a “cause célèbre” for other white supremacists, and worse yet, he was making these interactions, filled with primitive displays of dominance, in front of impressionable youths suction-cupped to their phones. But Eatherly was so extreme, he made even his idols blush. When speaking about Chud’s tactics about a week before the shooting, Nick Fuentes, the vocal America First white nationalist, said, “to me, it’s not nice.” Not only was it not nice, it was clear to anyone paying attention that it was just a matter of time before Chud got himself—or someone else—killed. All it would take was mouthing off to the wrong person. Evidently. From my outpost in Louisville, Kentucky, a town no stranger to armed xenophobic maniacs, I made four trips to Tennessee to report. At Eatherly’s May 21 bond hearing in Clarksville, I witnessed, firsthand, the spectacle of Chud’s white supremacist circus colliding with the analog Montgomery County court system. More importantly, it was where I’d meet Joshua Fox’s soft-spoken mother, Carolyn Smith, and ultimately gain unique access to Fox, who has been miraculously recovering from his wounds in a secret location and who has become all but an afterthought in this entire tragedy. One reason Fox’s side of the story has gone untold is that like Eatherly, Fox was gagged by Judge Poland from speaking about the case on social media or to the press. Which, as well as posing its own set of unique challenges that curtailed my reporting, is also a bizarre way to treat a victim of a crime. To put a fine point on it, his story and the current moment in our country scare the shit out of me not just because of who we are becoming, but who we have long been. It should scare you too. The digital commodification of the cult of identity—rooted in hate, with all its gradations, and managed by our billionaire tech overlords—has created irrevocable tribal divisions. Instead of challenging the oligarchs eager to turn us against each other, little men like Eatherly do their bidding, often destroying their own lives in the process. The story of Chud’s origin begins with a banal road-rage incident in February of 2024. He called a Black female motorist the N-word and then became infamous in his hometown of Clarksville. Digital archives of the incident, later compiled in an online documentary tracking his multiyear radicalization, tell the tale. Alexis Kayla, the daughter of the driver he smeared, and who was in the car at the time, took a photo of Eatherly holding up a cross and posted it on the local Facebook page “Idiots in cars Clarksville,” writing: “My mom is driving me to school right , this guy almost side swiped us !! so we went in a different lane and he came over and spit on my moms car ans i asked ‘whats your problem’ and he goes ‘n—r!!’ and held this up.” The post took off in the military town that sits on the Cumberland River, where Fort Campbell is based, and where Facebook shaming apparently still holds significant reputational sway. A local chapter of Black Lives Matter is said to have weighed in. Eatherly claimed to Proud Boys founder Gavin McInnes on his podcast Get Off My Lawn that the road-rage incident created a backlash in the community that led to him being fired from his construction job. After getting fired, Eatherly pivoted, creating his own remodeling business, named after his initials, DLE Contracting. But it got doxed, too, which hindered his self-employment prospects (its webpage now includes the caption “#White Owned”). Then sometime in 2025, after his baptism at a local Catholic church was allegedly canceled by his priest, and after the mother of his child became fearful for her son’s welfare after the family received death threats, Eatherly took the first major step toward becoming Chud: He filmed a Black woman storming through a Walmart, adding the commentary: “It’s the usual suspects … she’s chimping out!” Once the video went viral, it appears Eatherly realized he was on to something and soon created his alias, Chud the Builder. He began going around Clarksville saying the N-word on camera and creating a blitzkrieg of content. Explaining to the War Room broadcast on the far-right Infowars website, Eatherly whined: “They took my job. They took my son. And then they took my baptism.” Then he added: “This is America. You can’t tell me what I can say, and I can’t tell you what you can say.” It’s worth noting that Eatherly’s origin story comes only from Eatherly’s own lips. I have not been able to confirm why he was fired from his job, nor if his Baptism was canceled as he claims it was. And framing himself as a victim who has lost his job, son, and faith due to his convictions reads as much like branding and mythologizing as it does truth-telling. Nevertheless, as I researched his clips and visited Clarksville on my second reporting trip, it became clear that Eatherly’s online antics had metastasized like a pervasive cancer that spread throughout the physical city limits of his hometown. It makes sense that he ultimately took his racist roadshow to Nashville, an hour away—he could no longer shit where he ate. I encountered Chud’s footprint almost everywhere I went. Clarksville was a veritable Chudapalooza, from the woman at the front desk of my hotel on the outskirts of town to the first stop I made at a coffee shop near the courthouse downtown. The friendly baristas at the coffee shop—talented at pulling a cortado—smiled when I brought his name up in conversation. I explained that I was doing a national story on Eatherly; they tiptoed around the events they had witnessed firsthand, and deferred to the owner, who declined to elaborate and asked to be kept anonymous for fear of the press creating a backlash for their business. But you could tell they had a story: Chud had been banned from their establishment. While filming content around Nashville at the beginning of May, just days before Fox was shot, Chud’s ragebaiting became even more extreme and crossed a dangerous threshold. On May 4, Eatherly posted on his X account: “Keep encouraging your fellow blacks to assault me. When you see a chimp with their brains blown out on the time line just know yall did this to your own people.” On May 7, he tweeted: “Series finale is dead chimp on the pavement and you monkeys rioting when I walk free. Stay tuned.” On May 8, Eatherly posted a clip of himself bear-spraying a Black man who intentionally knocked Chud’s trademark cowboy hat out of his hands and onto the sidewalk along Broadway—the Music City’s renowned strip. As the man walks away after being sprayed, Chud hunts him down, shouting: “Let’s go get his ass. Heil Hitler, n—r!” The following evening, May 9, Eatherly and his cameraman dined at Bob’s Steak & Chop House in the Omni Hotel in downtown Nashville, at 250 Rep. John Lewis Way. Seated at a banquette table in the back of the restaurant, the pair ordered two entrees, drinks, and appetizers, totaling $371.55, and at some point during the meal, Eatherly began livestreaming. Sensing his presence is causing a disturbance—a partial video of his livestream was later uploaded to X—Chud attempts to sweet-talk the middle-aged Black maître d’, trying to convince her he’s not there to cause any “shenanigans” and that in fact he’s the victim of his own infamy. There’s a rapper who put a “$100,000 bounty” on his head, he complains, before adding that he’s “got good money to spend here.” The manager asks Eatherly to stop streaming, and Eatherly fails to comply, so the manager orders him to leave, at which point Eatherly refuses to pay for his meal and begins to heckle the brown-skinned service staff member standing by. (“Hey Pajeet, why don’t you go shit in the street,” he films himself saying.) As he exits, Chud turns to this worker, again, who follows him to the door: “Hey Pajeet, this is America. You’re in my country. You hear me? We’re going to call ICE on that one.” Later that evening on Broadway, Eatherly was arrested, charged with theft of $1,000 or less (for not paying for his meal), disorderly conduct, and resisting arrest. He was later released on a $5,000 bond, which paired with the $1,000 bond he was already out on for a harassment charge he incurred in Montgomery County on Nov. 18, 2025. During a recess at the May 21 bond hearing, just eight days after the shooting that had happened outside the same courthouse, I spoke at length with Carolyn Smith, Joshua Fox’s mother. Smith told me that Fox had called her from the ambulance immediately after the shooting to tell her he loved her, as if he knew he might die at any moment. But he didn’t. Fox had had parts of his spleen, pancreas, and small and large intestine removed, and yet his tubes and catheter were out and he was already walking with a walker. Doctors were amazed he’d survived. On the evening of June 1, she gave me another update, over text: “Josh is slowly recovering physically with some complications to his ribs being fractured from the shooting. Mentally is going to be more challenging.” Minutes later, after giving me her son’s number and him mine, Fox messaged me: “Hey Charlie this is Joshua fox my mom sent your information to me.” We communicated over text—I thanked him for being in touch, asked about his recovery, and tried to gauge his interest in doing a formal interview down the road. He seemed eager to speak. Though I’d offered to create a more secure channel to communicate, via Signal or Proton Mail, he declined. “Lol no I trust you,” he texted. “My mom trust you so you have [to] be a good person. Thank you I’ll be in touch soon.” We messaged one another while he recovered. I also submitted information requests to the county, tracked pending motions, communicated with the criminal clerk at the Montgomery County courthouse, reached out to new sources, texted with his mother, pitched this article to Slate, planned my next trip to central Tennessee, and wrote in spurts. Of course, I was intensely interested in Fox’s version of what happened when Eatherly shot him, but he wasn’t ready to discuss any of that. He also wasn’t legally able: At the tail end of the May 21 bond hearing, and said almost as an afterthought, Judge Poland would surprisingly broaden the scope of the gag order he’d imposed on Eatherly to also include the victim, the lead witness, who was recovering in a hospital. At the time, before commandeering a physical copy of the order, I was uncertain of its authority because of the offhanded way the judge had said it, but nevertheless assumed the likelihood of speaking with Fox was nil—and later, when we began communicating, it curtailed the conversations we had. Instead, I began to get a limited sense of his personality. Fox was thoughtful and easy to talk to. I learned that he’s a proud father of two boys and a girl, and two dogs named Nipsey and Scrap, whom he affectionately calls his “babies.” He’s also something of a mama’s boy: “She’s definitely a rock in my foundation.” I found him quirky, introspective, and slightly nerdy. He has a wry sense of humor. He said the weirdest conspiracy theory he’d heard in the wake of the shooting was that he’s a government plant. When I joked, “I didn’t realize I was communicating with the NSA,” without skipping a beat, he replied: “breaking news the fbi let a spy get shot 5 times!” He says he loves Batman, comics, and anime: “I had a crush on this girl who was into anime in middle school. So I watched it so I could talk to her and ended up liking it.” In high school, he was a choir kid, and though he can “listen to just about anything,” he prefers “west coast rap.” He’s also “a big time gamer,” where he is often subjected to racial slurs, including the N-word, from competitors. But he says he doesn’t think twice about what someone spouts behind a screen. In terms of temperament, what stood out, considering the context of our conversations, was how mild-mannered he seemed. Similar to his mother, who expressed her empathy toward certain members of the Eatherly clan in the weeks following the shooting (both to me and Clarksville Now), one of the initial subjects Fox brought up was Eatherly’s child. He wanted to know if I knew who the child’s mother was, because he’d been “wanting to reach out and send her something.” I told him I didn’t know yet but that I’d do some digging. He replied: “yea I been trying to find her since I was in the hospital but I figured it would be weird to ask publicly lol.” I asked him if he’d seen the post Eatherly had made prior to the shooting about how his child’s mother wants to change her son’s surname because of how Chud had tarnished it. He wrote back: “Yea I heard about it. That’s why I wanted to reach out, make sure she was straight.” Fox grew up a military brat. Born in South Carolina, he lived abroad on a base in Baumholder, Germany, as a child, spent an interregnum in Texas with his mother (and stepfather and multiple brothers and sisters), and then the lion’s share of his formative years in Clarksville, after moving there in 2003. He graduated from West Creek High in 2011. In January 2014, Fox enlisted in the Army. He said he wanted to “show my mom I could make it in the world.” He did his basic training at Fort Leonard Wood in Missouri, and served for over six years, including a year at Camp Carroll in South Korea. (He liked it there and enjoyed learning about different cultures, and, he said, there was a big Nigerian community.) Just after COVID hit, in June of 2020, he was discharged “under honorable conditions.” His mother told me that when he was released from the service, he suffered from multiple disabilities, including PTSD, depression, and anxiety. It’s worth noting here that Fox never served a day in combat, nor did he step foot in Afghanistan (contrary to what Eatherly’s defense attorney claimed at the bond hearing while trying to paint Fox as a highly trained and experienced killer). He did, however, tell me that if he was called up, he’d go into combat “today.” Also like many vets, he loves guns, and is excited, post-recovery, to “go hog hunting.” He’s realistic about his extensive recovery though—“I got a while before I fire anything that’s not on a video game lol,” he texted me once. Like his mother, he’s religious and grew up “heavy in the church.” It informs why he thinks he survived being shot five times point-blank: “I believe god saved me,” he said. “I mean I still ask why to this day but god definitely had something to do with it.” A few minutes later he added, “He probably gave me the good genes to survive lol … Guided the bullets or something.” His body—which was 160 pounds before the shooting (according to his mother) and has dropped to 130 pounds afterward (according to Fox)—is covered in tattoos, many of which he got while serving. He agreed with the characterization that his ink had become something of an addiction or coping mechanism, not unlike his past drinking. (He said when he drank, he drank Hennessy “straight,” and he often drank alone.) During his service, he experienced some type of severe trauma, which he would not discuss in any greater detail, that led him to get out of the military, and later pick up the bottle. He did explain that his best friend from the military died by suicide (and was not the first friend to do so). After that, he said, his mental health spiraled. Though we did not explore the trauma he experienced that led to his PTSD, he did describe in some detail the level of racism he experienced as an active-duty member. In our conversations, he was open about those experiences. Fox also maintains a Facebook page where he goes by Joshua Luvv and self-identifies as a “digital creator.” He mostly posts humorous memes, some of which explore racial themes, and has made several comments relating to the Eatherly case online on the account and referenced his own criminal history. When the shooting of a Black man becomes national news, an army of online sleuths pops up to uncover blemishes in his record, as if those past misdeeds could somehow justify the actions of the shooter. I’m not here to be a part of that process, but I did promise to tell his story, and this is part of it. Fox certainly knows what the Montgomery County Jail looks like from the inside. He is the first to admit this: “I just been arrested so many times, as my record show, so i been around for while” in their system. This was as deep as I would penetrate into his side of this story. We had a phone call scheduled to discuss the specifics of his history in Montgomery County, but after conferencing with his mother, it became clear I needed to give Fox some space, out of respect for his recovery and in anticipation of the legal ramifications surrounding this case, so we did not hold the call. Fox’s rap sheet—including two DUI-related convictions, two contempt-of-court convictions (guilty pleas before Judge H. Reid Poland III, the same judge who would later impose a gag order on him in the Eatherly case), and two domestic charges for which he was not indicted—dates almost entirely to the years following his 2020 military discharge, a period he described to me as one of significant personal crisis. But they are also serious charges. With that said, I thoroughly enjoyed speaking with Fox. But because of the remote nature of our dialog and the fact we did not meet in person, as we had initially planned, and because there were strict limits on what he could say, he still remains something of a mystery to me. I hope we will get a chance to meet once the trial runs its course. He will almost certainly take the stand if the case goes to trial, and I am eager to hear what he knew about Eatherly before they met and why, when they did, he chose to not back down. One of the more surprising junctures in our text thread came on June 3, the day Dalton Eatherly appeared before Judge William R. Goodman III in his circuit criminal courtroom, after the case was bound over to a grand jury. It was the most eventful preliminary hearing following the initial bond hearing—involving motions by the defense for the reduction of bond (denied), acceptance of bail source (denied), and return of his truck (granted). Unlike the first bond hearing, this was livestreamed. Goodman made his stance on the case clear early on: “Even in its best light” the judge said, “it’s a situation where an individual had a gun in a fistfight.” The big event was Alex Rosen taking the stand—the right-wing vigilante owner-operator of the controversial but incredibly popular Predator Poachers, a subscription and crowdfunded outfit that streams sting ops to entrap child sex predators across the country. Rosen, who is also infatuated with saying the N-word, was attempting to pay a portion of Eatherly’s $1 million bond. He was there to testify to his ability to do so, and he provided yet another spectacle. The heavyset, ginger-bearded, balding 26-year-old Houstonian has a 500,000-follower-strong X account, @iFightForKids, that lists his current city as “Retardville, USA.” He himself is out on probation for a harassment charge he garnered last year in a Steak ’n Shake, a business he is now permanently banned from entering. When the judge asked why he wanted to do this, Rosen answered: “I’m hoping to bond him out; you know, I myself am a free-speech absolutist and I just don’t think he should be in jail right now.” Which was a bit of a dubious reply considering words had not shot Joshua Fox, a gun had. I watched the livestream and read Rosen’s brazen tweets upon leaving the courtroom. Then I texted Fox to see if he’d watched. Of course he had. “Honestly,” Fox wrote, “a little upset because I was actually an Alex Rosen fan.” Lizard intestines! Joshua Fox liked Alex Rosen! Fox later clarified that he was more a fan of his work catching sexual predators and less up to speed on his free-speech absolutism. But still. At the first bond hearing, a $1 million bail seemed impossible for Eatherly to make, particularly because Poland had barred him from using any of the roughly $270,000 in post-shooting crowdfunding he’d digitally cadged on GiveSendGo from a horde of mostly anonymous benefactors. Later, bail looked even more untenable when Goodman added a more onerous condition: The backing had to come from 10 separate bail bondsmen, each of whom would have to agree to be on the hook for $100,000 a pop if Eatherly vanished. But in the times we live in, racists and resources have a way of finding each other: On Thursday, Aug. 6, Chud made bail for his three Nashville misdemeanors. Thanks to several shadowy backers, three days later, according to my source inside the courthouse, on Saturday, Aug. 8, at 12:35 p.m., Chud—still facing charges for attempted murder—walked out of the Montgomery County Jail. Like many of his right-wing predecessors, Dalton Eatherly has followed a common and clichéd path: Say or do something offensive. Get shunned by your community for your actions. Play the victim card. Claim freedom of speech. Ignore how free speech does not entail freedom from consequences. Fail to take responsibility for your actions. Get fired. Get arrested. Make a fool of yourself and act notorious enough on social media to crowdfund off your mistakes, and then do progressively more offensive things for money until you become a pariah and the cycle climaxes to a point of no return. Eatherly is now well past that point of no return. With his attempted murder case bound over, it could be just days before he’s formally arraigned. And yet he remains free. Well, as free as one is while yoked to a GPS monitor and curfew, stripped of his guns and passport, and barred from contacting the Fox family or speaking to the press or on social media about the case itself. But that hasn’t stopped him from using his notoriety to sell merch and pump his $CHUD scamcoin while winking to his online flock. Two days after he was released from jail, on Aug. 10, I watched Eatherly glide into Goodman’s courtroom in a three-piece suit for an unrelated harassment charge, becoming the first journalist in the country to document his freedom. As he exited, he knew he was being filmed, and he preened like a peacock to accentuate his comeuppance gimmick—perversely, I became an active participant in his sick ruse as my own footage gave his supporters new fodder. They would strip my video of its context, fail to cite its source, and spread more Chud propaganda that garnered nearly a million views (and blocked me in the process after I threatened copyright infringement). And still, the gag order remains—for both of them, technically. But while Chud continues to slyly profit off his alias, without saying a word about the shooting—yet—Joshua Fox is silenced almost completely and healing in a location he can’t disclose. Although not everyone is bound to hold their tongue. I met Jacob Fendley, Chud’s attorney, in Nashville at a June bond revocation hearing related to Eatherly’s alleged dine-and-dash at the steakhouse. While chit-chatting as my phone quietly recorded, I asked Fendley about the nature of gagging the person who was shot and who was not a party on trial. Fendley rejoined: “That’s really interesting that they want to gag people who aren’t really before the court. Courts typically have authority over the parties in a case. And so I’m not sure how effective it is to put a gag order on people who aren’t affiliated with the case.” I smiled and left the courthouse bouncing up and down as I contacted my editor in D.C.to let him know I’d commandeered a golden quote. I was so ecstatic at what I’d extracted—an implicit admission from Eatherly’s own attorney that it was borderline absurd to gag Fox—that I almost forgot I was waiting for an Uber ride to take me back to my honky-tonk-themed Best Western. I apologized to the driver for keeping him waiting, and he wanted to know why I was so ecstatic. I gave him a quick summation. Chud’s goofy name didn’t register with the man until I explained exactly who Eatherly was. Then, William Hyde, a middle-aged Black man born and bred in Nashville, looked dumbfounded—he too had been stricken by Chud. Several months earlier, while waiting to pick up a passenger, a man had come up to him, called him the N-word, and flashed his gun. When I asked William what he did in response, he replied, “I could have shot him,” because “I was strapped.” Instead, he told me, he shook his head and left. On June 7, Carolyn Smith texted me to let me know that the family had been in touch with an attorney from Ben Crump’s law firm. Crump is most famous for representing George Floyd’s family, though I came across him in Louisville when he was representing the family of Breonna Taylor. I asked Fox if he was ready for the spotlight, which I had witnessed in full force in Louisville. “Honestly no,” he replied. “Most people who know me personally know I’m more to myself. Goofy! But usually chill and to myself.” Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. If you value our work, please disable your ad blocker. News and Politics - Slate Magazine
Florida Man Loses. Florida Woman Wins.
2026-08-19

Florida Man Loses. Florida Woman Wins.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-08-19

Visit Original Website
This story is adapted from the Slatest newsletter. You can sign up here to get it in your inbox every day. Florida, that perpetual source of meme-worthy headlines and deep political disappointment for Democrats, held primary elections for both parties last night. The results were, frankly, a bit confusing. Democrats rejected an establishment-backed Senate contender in favor of a progressive—and simultaneously picked a former Republican to be their champion in the governor’s race. Republicans, meanwhile, voted down a string of ultra-extreme candidates in favor of candidates who are merely extreme. Let’s make sense of a head-spinning night. We’ve heard a lot about Democrats’ tug-of-war between the party’s centrist and progressive wings. Did either one win yesterday? Yes—they both did. Progressives scored their most impressive win of the night in the Democratic primary for U.S. Senate. Angie Nixon, a state representative who joined the Democratic Socialists of America this year, beat Alex Vindman, an Army veteran who testified during Donald Trump’s first impeachment hearing. Despite a 16-to-1 fundraising advantage and support from prominent senators, Vindman lost by 12 points. Nixon, a union organizer who supports Medicare for All, will face Republican Sen. Ashley Moody, who was appointed to the seat last year, in November. That sounds like a pretty big coup for the left! It was. But as with earlier Democratic primaries, Nixon’s win wasn’t a knockout blow in the party’s insider–outsider civil war. For one thing, Vindman moved to Florida just three years ago. For another, establishment candidates elsewhere in Florida easily fended off lefty rivals last night. Democrats’ nominee for governor, David Jolly, is so moderate that he wasn’t even a Democrat until last year. (He left the GOP in 2018, after criticizing Trump.) Meanwhile, Reps. Jared Moskowitz, a pro-Israel Democrat, and Debbie Wasserman Schultz, the former Democratic National Committee chairwoman, both beat democratic socialists in U.S. House primaries. So, as we wrote last week, the struggle for the party’s future seems set to continue. You can squint at last night’s results and wonder if Trump is losing his grip over the GOP electorate. Rep. Cory Mills, whom Trump endorsed even though the House Ethics Committee is investigating him for alleged sexual misconduct, lost his primary. (Trump, as is his wont, claimed today that he’d actually advised Mills “to get out of the Race, but he wouldn’t listen” and promptly endorsed the former TV anchor who beat him.) On the other side of the state, a former Trump official named Catalina Lauf also lost her bid for Congress despite the president’s endorsement. Rep. Byron Donalds, the president’s preferred nominee for governor, did win his primary, but with less than 50 percent of the vote. In some ways, these results fit a pattern. Other scandal-ridden Republicans and Trump-backed gubernatorial candidates have also struggled in or lost primaries this year, even in deep-red states. You mentioned more extreme candidates going down too? In a few notable races, Florida Republicans seemed to have lost their appetite for the far right. James Fishback, an alt-right, antisemitic troll whose gubernatorial campaign got oodles of national media attention, finished a dismal third. Dan Bilzerian, a manfluencer who blames Jews for America’s problems and posted an A.I.-generated campaign video praising Hitler, lost to Rep. Randy Fine (more on him in a minute), whom Bilzerian had called a “fat Jew.” And former Rep. Madison Cawthorn, who moved to Florida after North Carolina voters booted him from Congress amid sexual misconduct allegations and possible insider trading, lost his comeback bid. OK, but are the candidates Florida Republicans did nominate any less extreme? That depends on your point of view, but it’s true that several of them also hold some pretty-out-there (and often ugly) views. Donalds, who’s poised to become Florida’s first Black governor, is a former tea party activist who tried to help Trump overturn his 2020 defeat. Fine is an unrepentant Islamophobe who has called Palestinians “evil,” had his name inscribed on an artillery shell Israel planned to fire into Gaza, and labeled prominent Muslim Democrats terrorists. A spiral into ever-greater extremism doesn’t have to be the GOP’s future, but it’s also possible that voters are simply delaying the inevitable. Fishback, who has described Donalds using terms like DEI hire and slave, won precincts close to major college campuses, which suggests that his Groyper candidacy appealed to young conservatives (even as the number of people who voted Republican in those places was relatively small). Last night might not be the last we see of him. But does any of this infighting on both sides really matter? Florida seems to have swung pretty far to the right lately, so shouldn’t we still expect Republicans to dominate in November? It’s a fair question. No other state this century has so consistently let Democrats down. In 2000, the Sunshine State cost Al Gore the presidency (with an assist from the Supreme Court). Barack Obama won Florida twice, but it bucked the blue wave of 2018, narrowly rejecting Democrats for Senate and governor, and sped further rightward in 2022. Its voters thrice opted for Trump, giving him greater margins of victory each time. What does all that mean for this fall? Republicans aggressively gerrymandered Florida’s congressional map earlier this year, redrawing districts in a bid to eliminate four Democrats. Political observers also expect two of the Democrats running statewide this year, Jolly for governor and Nixon for Senate, to lose. But under the hood, Democrats got some signals that they’re entering peak campaign season on stronger footing even in this onetime swing state that has repeatedly given them false hope. The party flipped multiple school board seats and a Jacksonville City Council seat from Republicans last night. So, despite the GOP’s clear advantages in this year’s marquee races, 2026 could be Democrats’ best chance in a while to claw back at least some power in the nation’s third-most-populous state. With another Election Day gone, it’s time to turn your attention to tonight—specifically, how to enjoy it. My colleagues recommend: Bad news for Trump: If the current state of American politics is getting you down, this one might cheer you up: Gen Z voters are souring on the president—and 23-year-old podcaster Adam Mockler sees a prime opportunity there for Democrats. He spoke to Slate’s Mary Harris about the lessons the party should be learning right now. (You can also watch a video version of this interview on YouTube.) Historical brain teasers: Do you know which pharaoh the Great Pyramid of Giza was constructed for? How about which U.S. president was nicknamed “His Fraudulency” by critics who believed he had stolen the election from Samuel J. Tilden? Test your knowledge of historical trivia with our daily quiz. A defense of yelling in restaurants: It’s a forbidden behavior in today’s restaurants—but Amanda Albee spoke to chefs who say it massively improved their careers. Fans of Gordon Ramsay shows and The Bear might be especially interested in this one. A deep dive that might change how you see bar soap: The humble bar of soap used to define the American way. Now it’s dying. In a sweeping, very entertaining investigative account, Dan Kois delves into the history and benefits of bar soap and why it’s worth fighting for. If you, too, have abandoned bars of soap in your bathroom, Dan’s spirited and incredibly thorough reporting might just make you think twice the next time you stock up. And on that squeaky-clean note, I take my leave. See you tomorrow! Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. If you value our work, please disable your ad blocker. NetBSD 11 lands with RISC-V support and lightning-fast VM boots
2026-08-20

NetBSD 11 lands with RISC-V support and lightning-fast VM boots

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-08-20

Visit Original Website
Venerable Unix-like remains reassuringly old-school, if you're willing to put in the work NetBSD's 19th major release adds 64-bit RISC-V support, improved Linux compatibility, and a MICROVM kernel that can boot in milliseconds. NetBSD 11.0 arrived at the end of July, a little over two years after the project celebrated its 30th birthday with the release of version 10.0. We looked at what was coming last year. After multiple release candidates, the final version is here, and we tried it. NetBSD is a stable, somewhat minimalist, and relatively slow-moving project. If you're willing to put some work in, this arguably makes it a refreshing change from the ever-accelerating techbro world of Linux. As The Register noted of Linux 7.2 earlier this week, it is a huge release: the increasingly AI-assisted development process is rapidly adding features and expanding Rust support while shedding compatibility with older hardware. NetBSD is not static, though. Version 11.0 nonetheless brings some significant additions. In addition to nine platforms with Tier I support, including x86-32 and x86-64, Arm32, Arm64, MIPS, and PowerPC, it also has an impressive 52 platforms with Tier II support. Version 11 brings support for 64-bit RISC-V, greater compliance with POSIX.1-2024 and C23, improved Linux system-call compatibility, and better operation under Xen and QEMU. A new dedicated MICROVM kernel supports both 32-bit and 64-bit x86 guests and can boot in about 10 milliseconds on 2020-era processors, according to the project. The new RISC-V port supports QEMU, while the new virt68k port runs the Motorola 68000 version of NetBSD under QEMU using paravirtualized devices. There's preliminary support for Qualcomm Snapdragon X Elite, meaning PC-like Arm64 systems, and the npf firewall has been improved. NetBSD is a general-purpose OS. It tends not to attract as much attention as the other BSDs, perhaps partly because they have more clearly defined roles. FreeBSD is a server-oriented OS with ZFS and rich container support, including Linux OCI containers… but even the latest version has a text-only installer. If you want a GUI on FreeBSD, you must install it yourself. DragonflyBSD is the x86-64-only experimental research OS. OpenBSD is the extremely minimalist, very portable, and very secure OS, to the extent that it doesn't even support Bluetooth. NetBSD's strength is that it is not confined to any one of these roles. It can run as a Xen host or guest, and its new MICROVM kernel is designed for lightweight, fast-starting x86 VMs. The external SmolBSD project can also help users build customized NetBSD VM images. It's a capable server OS: the SDF public-access Unix system mostly runs on NetBSD. It can run Linux binaries, and as the compat_linux manual page describes, the NetBSD repositories contain cut-down sets of openSUSE libraries to help build a Linux environment. The repos also have packages of many of the usual FOSS suspects, such as Firefox and even WINE. There is a choice of desktop environments, including LXDE, LXQt, KDE Plasma, MATE, and GNOME, along with numerous more minimal window managers. Since NetBSD 11.0 was released, for instance, it got a newer version of Enlightenment. The packaged versions often lag behind the latest upstream releases, but they do the job. The classic Common Desktop Environment is there, but despite multiple helpful posts on the NetBSD-users mailing list, we were unable to get it working. However, NetBSD requires more manual work than a typical 21st-century Linux distribution. Its base package tools include pkg_add, but users wanting higher-level features such as repository searches and convenient upgrades may prefer to install pkgin. Handily, the text-mode installer, called sysinst, is installed with the rest of the OS. You can run it after installation to do any steps you might have forgotten or skipped, such as configuring your network connection, adding user accounts, or indeed, installing the package manager. You need to become comfortable editing configuration files and scripts, including enabling services manually in /etc/rc.conf. You will also have to rely on FOSS drivers, as very few vendors produce proprietary drivers for NetBSD. If you have a high-end graphics card and want the latest PC games, forget about it – this is not the OS for you. But if you want to build your Unix muscles and really learn your way around a Unix box, this is a good option. This will teach you a lot more than getting Arch Linux installed and tweaking its desktop settings. If you want to learn about contributing code and patches, it also has one of the most welcoming communities we've encountered in years. ® Venerable Unix-like remains reassuringly old-school, if you're willing to put in the work A spoonful of encryption helps the malware go down PARTNER CONTENT: Platform engineering won the argument. Now it has to grow up fast and evolve for the AI era. Stolen details range from contact information to household finances and withholding rates The good news? £37M SAP overhaul could extend Capgemini's run with UK tax collector to 28 years
2026-08-20

£37M SAP overhaul could extend Capgemini's run with UK tax collector to 28 years

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-08-20

Visit Original Website
UK tax collector insists latest deal followed fair and competitive procurement The UK's tax collector has awarded Capgemini a £37 million contract to help migrate a critical tax system from SAP's legacy ECC platform to S/4HANA, extending a supplier relationship that began in 2004. HM Revenue & Customs (HMRC) has hired the French IT services company to move the Enterprise Tax Management Platform (ETMP) from SAP ECC 6.0 to its successor, S/4HANA, a completely re-engineered application platform. The system provides 40,000 users with returns processing, tax accounting, payments, and data management capabilities, and handles more than £800 billion in tax revenue and payments annually. The contract runs until 2032, potentially extending Capgemini's involvement with HMRC's tax systems to 28 years. Capgemini became HMRC's lead technology supplier in 2004 under the Aspire contract, with Fujitsu and Accenture serving as subcontractors. In 2014, the National Audit Office (NAO) said HMRC was set to spend £10.4 billion on the contract, compared to the £4.1 billion estimate used to evaluate Capgemini's bid. In 2022, Capgemini won a £51 million, three-year contract to provide run and change services for ETMP and Enterprise Operations (EOPS). In 2024, the French supplier again won the contract to provide run and change services for ETMP and EOPS. The five-year agreement, which runs until June 2029, is valued at between £403 million and £574 million. HMRC has previously denied that its later ETMP contracts with Capgemini were extensions of Aspire, which formally ended in 2017, although some services continued until 2020. Separately, Capgemini won a Contact Centre as a Service contract worth up to £600 million over ten years. The Register understands there were several bidders for the new ETMP migration deal, but HMRC officials argued the details were commercially sensitive. They said HMRC took steps to ensure a fair and transparent procurement and a level playing field for all bidders. The competitors came from Lot 3 of the Digital & Legacy Application Services (DALAS) framework, which lists Accenture, Atos, Capgemini, CGI, Cognizant, and IBM as suppliers. Ethical wall arrangements and non-disclosure agreements were put in place to prevent commercially sensitive information from being shared with bidders already working for HMRC through other contracts, officials said. In January, HMRC awarded SAP a £275 million contract for S/4HANA without competition, saying in the award notice that the German software giant was the "only supplier with sovereign capability." In June, Capgemini said it had become an SAP Sovereign Cloud Partner for the UK. Andy Allen, a relationship director at Capgemini, later said the company would work with Invenio to support ETMP's migration to S/4HANA on SAP's UK Sovereign Cloud. "It's a complex and important programme, helping to ensure continuity, resilience and a platform fit for the future," he said. A Capgemini case study published in 2012 (now removed but archived here) said that ETMP was one of the projects it ran under Aspire. It also migrated Stamp Duty Land Tax onto the system. In 2014, the NAO said [PDF] Aspire had delivered improvements fundamental to HMRC's administration of tax, but found that the department had not evaluated the contract's overall strengths and weaknesses. The watchdog estimated that HMRC would spend £10.4 billion over the contract's lifetime, compared with the £4.1 billion figure used to assess Capgemini's bid. It said Capgemini and Fujitsu had earned considerably more profit than initially modeled, while pressure to cut costs had led HMRC to surrender some negotiating power. A parliamentary committee subsequently accused suppliers of outmaneuvering HMRC at key points. It said [PDF] Aspire cost £7.9 billion between July 2004 and March 2014 and generated £1.2 billion in combined profit for Capgemini and Fujitsu, equivalent to a margin of 15.8 percent. In January 2025, research by The Register and public sector spending specialist Tussell found that HMRC had awarded £3.8 billion in contracts since 2020 to technology suppliers previously involved in Aspire. At the time, an HMRC spokesperson told The Register: "We follow government procurement rules when awarding contracts and look for solutions that will improve services and get the best deals for taxpayers. For transparency, we publish contract awards along with contract variations." www.theregister.com - Articles
Grok chat duped into swallowing injected instructions
2026-08-20

Grok chat duped into swallowing injected instructions

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-08-20

Visit Original Website
A spoonful of encryption helps the malware go down xAI's Grok web chat agent is currently vulnerable to a novel form of prompt injection, according to security researchers with Adversa AI. The technique allows an attacker to create a web page poisoned with malicious instructions that induce an AI model summarizing the page to carry out harmful actions. That describes a well-known attack known as indirect prompt injection. Frontier AI models have become better at dealing with such attempts through existing guardrails, though the issue is far from resolved. Adversa's approach comes with a twist: It relies on encrypted malicious instructions, which attackers place on a web page alongside an encryption key. The model guardrail scanner – an input filter – can't read the encrypted text despite the presence of the key. The scanner therefore passes it on to the model, which can use the key to decrypt the instructions. The model then carries out instructions in the decrypted text as would be the case in any other indirect prompt injection attack. Adversera calls its method "cryptographic context injection." "An attacker ships ciphertext along with the key material and an instruction to decrypt it, and the model runs that decryption inside its own code execution sandbox," wrote Rony Utevsky, lead researcher at Adversa AI, in a blog post. "Everything a guardrail’s scanner would need is right there on the page, but recovering the plaintext means running PBKDF2 and AES-256-GCM, which no content classifier does at inspection time." Other attacks on AI models have relied on cipher-based evasion, such as base64 encoding. But because these are weak and reversible cipher mechanisms, models can decode them natively from their own training data, Utevsky said. That doesn't work for strong encryption, so decryption must be done through the code execution runtime. The runtime thus becomes a mechanism for trust laundering – the model trusts its own output, namely the malicious instructions that it decrypted. In a proof-of-concept demo, Adversa shows how the technique can be used to exfiltrate the victim's chat history with Grok.com. The attack transmits the user’s name, coarse location, subscription tier, and the full set of the user’s prompts in the conversation by appending them to a URL as parameters. Other models may be vulnerable to varying degrees. With Google's Gemini public chat interface (gemini.google.com), Utevsky told The Register, the Grok scenario doesn't work because Gemini doesn't provide Python with access to external websites. "So it's useful only to sneak bad questions and answers past guardrails," he explained. When Adversa tested cryptographic context injection on Gemini, they were able to get the model to produce content that normally would be blocked by safety filters – instructions for how to build an incendiary weapon. xAI, according to Utevsky, was informed about the attack on June 3, 2026, directly and through its HackerOne bug bounty program. We're told xAI acknowledged the report but did not provide a mitigation timeline. Additional attempts to raise the issue are said to have occurred on August 4 and August 10. As of August 19, we're told, the technique still worked on Grok.com. SpaceX, which acquired xAI earlier this year, did not respond to a request for comment. Google was not informed of the attack, according to Utevsky, because it considers jailbreaks – bypassing guardrails to make models emit harmful content – to be out of scope for its vulnerability disclosure program. Nonetheless, the attack success rate against Gemini declined significantly by August, which Utevsky suggests could be due to filter updates, model version changes, or both. Asked whether cryptographic context injection can be compared to return oriented programming (ROP) in terms of the way it assembles attack gadgets from separately harmless parts of stored memory, Utevsky said, "The ROP analogy is close, though ROP works that way out of necessity – the attacker can't inject code at all, so they're stuck reusing gadgets already in memory. "Same shape here otherwise. A static guardrail reads text one artifact at a time. If no single artifact is harmful, they all pass, and the malicious meaning appears only once the runtime assembles them. And guardrails can't see into the runtime. But Utevsky added that cryptographic context injection is more open than ROP. "The agent's runtime is a general-purpose interpreter, so the pieces are arbitrary," he explained. "You could split an instruction across several encrypted fragments, fetched pages, or tool outputs, none meaningful in isolation, and let the runtime concatenate them. We haven't demonstrated that, but nothing rules it out. "So yes, cryptographic context injection is one kind of link, not necessarily the whole chain. "The moment agents got code and tools, the guardrail's unit of inspection (a string) stopped being the unit of action (a composed, executed program). This is a big playing field. Our earlier SymJack attack reached the same place through symlinks and shell behavior. Encryption adds another trick to the game." ® Venerable Unix-like remains reassuringly old-school, if you're willing to put in the work A spoonful of encryption helps the malware go down PARTNER CONTENT: Platform engineering won the argument. French tax authority says break-in exposed data of 600K, including some private messages
2026-08-20

French tax authority says break-in exposed data of 600K, including some private messages

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-08-20

Visit Original Website
Stolen details range from contact information to household finances and withholding rates France's tax authority says attackers may have stolen the contents of messages exchanged with hundreds of taxpayers during the data raid it confirmed last week. In an update published this week, the General Directorate of Public Finances (DGFiP) said lists of messages exchanged with the authority were exposed. For around 250 people, the compromised information also included the messages themselves. Slightly more than 350,000 individuals were affected. The other exposed data included tax identification numbers, marital status, email and postal addresses, and phone numbers. Tax records also exposed details such as household composition, number of dependents, family quotient, reference tax income, and withholding rates. Beyond the personal and tax information, DGFiP said the other affected datasets contained information that was already publicly available. For approximately 250,000 businesses and professionals, the affected data was limited to company names and SIREN numbers, the unique nine-digit identifiers assigned to French businesses. The compromised cadastral data was limited to property addresses and dimensions, which DGFiP said were already publicly available. DGFiP said it was notifying affected taxpayers by email or post this week. DGFiP's latest FAQ pegs the total number of affected parties at roughly 600,000. That appears lower than the 678,000 "individuals and professionals" DGFiP said were affected last week, shortly after the alleged cybercriminal behind the attack, "ZeroBytes," claimed to have stolen data belonging to more than 2 million. The authority did not explain the discrepancy. The notifications warn that criminals could use the stolen details to make phishing attempts appear more convincing. DGFiP highlighted impersonation attempts, CEO fraud, and scams involving bogus bank advisers as possible follow-on attacks. The authority said it would never ask taxpayers to provide sensitive information such as PINs or identity documents by phone, text message, or email, and would request such material only through its secure portal. Separately, the tax authority disclosed a "technical vulnerability" in the government's Vacant Successions Portal (PSV), which is used to search for estates without known heirs. DGFiP suspended the service after discovering the flaw. It said there was no evidence so far that personal data had leaked, although its investigation into possible exposure of applicants' details continues. The incident adds to a torrid year for cybersecurity across France's public sector. In February, the finance ministry, which oversees DGFiP, 'fessed up to an intrusion into a database containing citizens' bank details that affected 1.2 million people. The Health Ministry confirmed in March that 15.8 million administrative files, 165,000 of which contained doctors' notes, were stolen during an attack on healthtech company Cegedim Santé. A month later, a 15-year-old allegedly carried out an attack on France Titres, which handles the country's identity documents. The attacker claimed the breach affected between 18 million and 19 million people. In June, France also began probing an alleged breach of Tchap, the government's encrypted messaging platform, after attackers claimed to have accessed 73,000 user accounts, 643,000 messages, and nearly 60,000 media files. ® Venerable Unix-like remains reassuringly old-school, if you're willing to put in the work A spoonful of encryption helps the malware go down PARTNER CONTENT: Platform engineering won the argument. OpenAI glitch locks out vetted cyber researchers – and some can't get back in
2026-08-20

OpenAI glitch locks out vetted cyber researchers – and some can't get back in

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-08-20

Visit Original Website
Affected users say support cannot restore their previous approval or override the new decision OpenAI says a technical stuff-up booted some vetted security researchers out of its Trusted Access for Cyber (TAC) program, only for its recovery process to decide some of them aren't welcome back. The problem surfaced this week when participants reported that their previously approved status had vanished without warning. OpenAI's cyber verification page instead invited them to "Start verification" as though they had never been cleared. Some also found that Daybreak Blue, an access tier offering vetted cyber defenders expanded capabilities, had disappeared from Codex Desktop and the command-line interface. OpenAI has since acknowledged the problem. Responding to complaints on its community forum, the company said "a limited set of users" had lost Daybreak Blue access because of a technical issue and would need to complete verification again. "This isn't the experience we want to deliver, and we're making sure the issue doesn't happen again," OpenAI said. Affected users were told to look for an email titled "Action required: Reverify your Daybreak Blue access," containing instructions for getting back through the door. For some, however, it appears to remain shut. One researcher said they followed the instructions and selected "Start verification," only to be told that the process could not begin because their account was ineligible. Another participant said they had held Daybreak Blue access for more than a month before losing it in the technical snafu. After OpenAI support told them to reapply, the verification system reportedly said it could not verify their identity or that the account was currently ineligible. According to their account of a subsequent exchange with support, OpenAI could neither reset the verification state nor restore or override the previous approval. TAC gives security professionals access to advanced cyber capabilities that are more restricted for other users. Applicants must provide identification and undergo verification before getting access. The aim is to give legitimate defenders more powerful tools for finding and fixing vulnerabilities while restricting access for those with less wholesome intentions. Anthropic operates a comparable scheme called the Cyber Verification Program. That makes this more than an option mysteriously vanishing from a menu. OpenAI had already vetted these researchers – then its own technical problem kicked them out, and its verification system apparently changed its mind. OpenAI didn't respond to The Register's questions about the number of affected users, the cause of the removals, why some previously approved researchers are now deemed ineligible, or whether it can restore access when re-verification fails. It instead directed us to a statement posted on X that blamed the access removals on a "technical issue," saying only a limited number of users were affected, and telling those users they would need to re-verify. For now, some of OpenAI's trusted cyber researchers have discovered an unexpected vulnerability: the front door. ® Venerable Unix-like remains reassuringly old-school, if you're willing to put in the work A spoonful of encryption helps the malware go down PARTNER CONTENT: Platform engineering won the argument. Microsoft probes reports of games taking exception to Windows 11's August update

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-08-20

Visit Original Website
Affected titles crash, freeze, or restart PCs while Redmond works out whether it is to blame Microsoft is investigating reports of games crashing after users installed August's Patch Tuesday update for Windows 11. The company acknowledged the reports on its Release Health page. Affected games may become unresponsive, close unexpectedly, throw an "EXCEPTION_ACCESS_VIOLATION" error, or trigger an unexpected device restart. Reports of the problem followed the release of KB5121003 for Windows 11 24H2 and 25H2. The KB5121003 support page still lists no known issues, but Microsoft has separately confirmed on Release Health that it is investigating the game crash reports. The problem does not affect every title. Microsoft named ARC Raiders, MARVEL Tōkon: Fighting Souls, and The Finals among those generating reports. The Windows giant has not accepted responsibility, writing: "We are presently investigating to determine if this is an issue caused by Microsoft." Users have theorized that tighter kernel handle validation may have exposed problems in third-party drivers that Windows previously tolerated, causing crashes when code ventures where it should not. That might be good for security, but less so for software that depended on the previous behavior. Microsoft has not confirmed the theory. August's Patch Tuesday was a big one, addressing 421 Microsoft CVEs, including 236 affecting Windows. As such, uninstalling it as a workaround is probably not a good idea. The reports come as Microsoft tightens its treatment of old and incompatible kernel drivers. In March, it removed trust for kernel drivers that hadn't been through the Windows Hardware Compatibility Program (WHCP) to lock down the Windows kernel further. Microsoft has also launched its Driver Quality Initiative (DQI) to improve operating system stability. The crashes remain under investigation, and Microsoft has asked affected users for feedback. ® Venerable Unix-like remains reassuringly old-school, if you're willing to put in the work A spoonful of encryption helps the malware go down PARTNER CONTENT: Platform engineering won the argument.
